## Page 9


CHAPTER
ONE
INTRODUCTION
The derived category of an abelian category was introduced by Grothendieck and
Verdier in the 1960s [Ver96b]. It was originally introduced as a natural setting
for doing homological algebra, and is very useful for studying derived functors.
This has featured prominently in algebraic geometry, an area where homological
algebra is used extensively. Here, one often looks at Db(X), the derived category
of coherent sheaves on a variety X.
Later, the derived category was studied as an invariant of the variety X, for
example by Bondal, Mukai and Orlov [Muk81, Orl97, Orl02, BO01]. It turns out
that many geometric properties of X are captured by its derived category. For ex-
ample, if two smooth projective varieties X and Y over C have equivalent derived
categories (for us, this will always mean a C-linear additive exact equivalence of
triangulated categories Db(X) ≃Db(Y )), then they have the same dimension
and their canonical bundles have the same order [Huy06, Proposition 4.1]. In
fact, Bondal and Orlov proved that if X has either ample or anti-ample canoni-
cal bundle and Db(X) ≃Db(Y ), then X and Y are isomorphic. Similarly, if X
and Y are derived equivalent smooth projective curves over C, then X and Y are
isomorphic [Huy06, Corollary 5.46].
Despite the above examples, the derived category of a smooth projective vari-
ety X does not recover X in general. The earliest example of two non-isomorphic
varieties with equivalent derived categories comes from Mukai [Muk81], who
showed that for every abelian variety X, there is an equivalence Db(X) ≃Db(X∗),
where X∗denotes the dual abelian variety of X.
Another interesting example in this direction comes from K3 surfaces. For
a K3 surface X, after identifying H4(X; Z) with Z, the cohomology H•(X; Z)
becomes a lattice, where one defines the pairing ⟨−, −⟩by letting ⟨x, y⟩be the
degree 4 part of the cup product x ∪y for x, y ∈H•(X; Z).
Mukai [Muk87]


## Page 10


2
Introduction
equipped this lattice with the weight two Hodge structure which extends the
weight two Hodge structure of H2(X; Z), and where H0(X; Z) and H4(X; Z) are
algebraic (i.e. of type (1, 1)). The resulting structure is called the Mukai lattice
of X. Orlov [Orl97] then showed that two K3 surfaces X and Y are derived
equivalent if and only if there is a Hodge isometry between their Mukai lattices,
a result which is now known as the derived Torelli theorem.
Despite the fact that two derived equivalent varieties need not be isomorphic,
they do still share a lot of structure. An important result in this direction comes
from Orlov [Orl02], who showed that every equivalence Φ: Db(X)
∼
→Db(Y )
for smooth projective varieties X and Y is given by a so-called Fourier-Mukai
transform. If the varieties X and Y are defined over the complex numbers, one
obtains from this an induced isomorphism on cohomology
ΦH : H•(X; Q) →H•(Y ; Q),
where H•(X; Q) denotes the singular cohomology of the analytification X(C)an.
The isomorphism ΦH does not need to respect the grading. However, it does
preserve the Hodge grading, in the sense that for every n we have (after extending
scalars to C and using the Hodge decomposition):

Roughly, ΦH respects the columns of the Hodge diamond, but not the rows. It
is then natural to ask whether there could also exist an isomorphism H•(X; Q) →
H•(Y ; Q) which preserves the grading, and is an isomorphism of Hodge structures
in each degree. The following was conjectured by Orlov:
Conjecture 1.1 (Orlov [Orl05]). Let X and Y be two smooth projective varieties
over C, and assume that there exists an equivalence of triangulated categories
Db(X) ≃Db(Y ). Then for every i there is an isomorphism of Hodge structures
Hi(X; Q) ∼= Hi(Y ; Q).
In fact, Orlov conjectured something even stronger, namely that derived
equivalent varieties have isomorphic motives:
Conjecture 1.2 (Orlov [Orl05]). Let X and Y be two smooth projective vari-
eties over a field k, and assume that there exists an equivalence of triangulated
categories Db(X) ≃Db(Y ). Then the rational Chow motives of X and Y are
isomorphic.
A weaker form of Conjecture 1.1 just asks for equality of the Hodge numbers
hp,q(X) = hp,q(Y ), where hp,q(X) = dim Hq(X, Ωp
X). The invariance of Hodge


## Page 11


3
numbers under derived equivalences would also follow from conjectures made by
Kontsevich in the context of mirror symmetry [Kon95]. Equation (1.1) implies
that for every n the sum P
q−p=n hp,q is a derived invariant, which is some evi-
dence towards this conjecture. It should be noted that the invariance of Hodge
numbers is not true in positive characteristic [ABP23].
These conjectures are already known in various cases, which we summarize
here:
1. If X and Y are smooth projective curves over C, or X has (anti)-ample
canonical bundle, and Db(X) ≃Db(Y ), then we have seen above that
X ∼= Y , so the conjectures are trivially true.
2. If X and Y are abelian varieties with duals X∗and Y ∗respectively, and
Db(X) ≃Db(Y ), then there is an isomorphism X×X∗∼= Y ×Y ∗by [Orl02].
Since X∗and X are isogenous, there is an isomorphism of weight 1 Hodge
structures H1(X; Q) ∼= H1(X∗; Q). The projectivity of X and Y implies
that the Hodge structures on their cohomology are polarizable, and hence
split as a direct sum of their simple sub-Hodge structures. It follows that
there is an isomorphism of weight 1 Hodge structures H1(X; Q) ∼= H1(Y ; Q),
and taking the exterior power of this isomorphism gives an isomorphism
H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Y ; Q) which respects the grading and is an isomorphism of
Hodge structures in each degree.
3. If X and Y are smooth projective K3 surfaces and Φ: Db(X)
∼
→Db(Y ) is
an equivalence, then we saw above that there is a not necessarily graded
isomorphism ΦH : H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Y ; Q) as above. By applying a Tate twist
to the cohomology of X in degrees 0 and 4, we can give the cohomology
a weight 2 Hodge structure, and this is then isomorphic to Q(−1)⊕2 ⊕
H2(X; Q). Hence ΦH gives an isomorphism of weight 2 Hodge structures
Q(−1)⊕2 ⊕H2(X; Q) ∼= Q(−1)⊕2 ⊕H2(Y ; Q).
Since the category of polarizable weight 2 Hodge structures is semisimple,
we can cancel the factors Q(−1)⊕2, and this proves Conjecture 1.1 for K3
surfaces.
Note that this example not only works for K3 surfaces, but in fact for all
surfaces with h1 = 0, and can even be generalized to all smooth projective
surfaces.
4. The above example was generalized by Taelman [Tae23] to hyperkähler
varieties, who showed that Conjecture 1.1 holds if X and Y are derived
equivalent hyperkähler varieties.

5. If X and Y are derived equivalent smooth projective varieties over C, then
h0,1(X) = h0,1(Y ) by [PS11, Corollary B].
6. The above result also implies that if X and Y are smooth projective varieties
over C of dimension at most 3 and Db(X) ≃Db(Y ), then X and Y have
the same Hodge numbers, see [PS11, Corollary C].
Since Conjecture 1.1 is known for varieties with ample or anti-ample canoni-
cal bundle, a logical next step is to try to prove the conjecture for more general
varieties with trivial canonical bundle other than abelian varieties or hyperkähler
varieties. By the Beauville-Bogomolov decomposition [Bea83b], any smooth pro-
jective complex variety X with trivial canonical bundle admits an étale covering

where A is an abelian variety, the Xi are hyperkähler varieties and the Yj are
strict Calabi-Yau varieties. One could hope that a derived equivalence between
two varieties with trivial canonical bundle can be understood in terms of these
simpler factors.
Huybrechts and Nieper-Wisskirchen [HNW11] obtained some
results in this direction. As a first question, one may wonder whether the derived
category can distinguish between the three different kinds of factors that occur.
In this direction, they prove the following:
Theorem 1.3 ([HNW11, Theorem 0.4]). Let X and Y be two derived equivalent
smooth projective varieties. If X is either an abelian or a hyperkähler variety,
then Y is of the same type.
Moreover, when considering more than one factor in the Beauville-Bogomolov
decomposition, they obtain the following result:
Theorem 1.4 ([HNW11, Proposition 4.11]). Let X1, . . . , Xm and X′

be hyperkähler varieties, and assume that Db(Q Xi) ≃Db(Q X′

is a bijection σ: {1, . . . , m} →{1, . . . , n} such that dim Xi = dim X′

σ(i)) for all i.
Given these two theorems, there are several interesting paths to explore. On
the one hand, since Conjecture 1.1 is already known for the abelian and hyper-
kähler factors, one could try to prove it for the strict Calabi-Yau factors. Another
possible avenue of research is to combine the results obtained so far, and study
what happens if the Beauville-Bogomolov decomposition of X is nontrivial, for
example if there is a nontrivial étale covering, or if there are several factors in the
Beauville-Bogomolov decomposition of X. Note that the Beauville-Bogomolov
decomposition is not unique, but that there is not too much variation in the pos-
sible étale covers of a given variety with trivial canonical bundle. See Section 2.5
for a summary of some results of Beauville in this direction.


## Page 13


5
This thesis makes contributions in the second direction. Our main result is
that Conjecture 1.1 holds for many varieties which admit a holomorphic sym-
plectic form. If X is a smooth projective variety which admits a holomorphic
symplectic form, its Beauville-Bogomolov covering (1.2) does not contain any
strict Calabi-Yau factors, so we only have to deal with the abelian and hyperkäh-
ler factors. The reason that we restrict to varieties with a holomorphic symplectic
form is that we can use a theorem from Taelman [Tae23], which also underlies his
proof of Conjecture 1.1 for hyperkähler varieties. This result is about the derived
invariance of the so-called LLV Lie algebra that can be associated to a smooth
projective variety. The LLV algebra of a smooth projective variety X, denoted by
llv(X; Q), is a semisimple Lie algebra over Q, which comes with a representation
on H•(X; Q). In Chapter 3, we discuss the LLV algebra in more detail.
The Lie algebra llv(X; Q) can be computed in many cases. For example, the
definition of the Mukai lattice of a K3 surface can be generalized to obtain a
Mukai lattice eH(X; Q) of a hyperkähler variety X, and there is an isomorphism
llv(X; Q) ∼= so(eH(X; Q)), see [LL97], [Ver95] and [Sol21]. Taelman [Tae23] proved
that for symplectic varieties, the LLV algebra, together with its representation
on cohomology, is a derived invariant:
Theorem 1.5 ([Tae23, Theorem A]). Let X and Y be two smooth projective vari-
eties over C, both admitting a holomorphic symplectic form, and let Φ: Db(X) ≃
Db(Y ) be an equivalence of triangulated categories.
Then Φ induces an iso-
morphism of LLV algebras Φllv : llv(X; Q) →llv(Y ; Q), with the property that
ΦH : H•(X; Q) →H•(Y ; Q) is equivariant with respect to the actions of the LLV
algebras on the cohomology.
The derived invariance of the LLV algebra can be used to prove the derived
invariance of other invariants of X. For example, we show in Section 4.4 how this
result, together with some computations of the LLV algebra and its representation
on cohomology, can be used to give an alternative proof of Theorem 1.4.
Main results
We now discuss the main results of this thesis, all of which build on Theorem 1.5.
First, we will prove Orlov’s conjecture for varieties admitting a holomorphic sym-
plectic form, under some assumptions on their LLV algebra. Take an integer
n ≥1. We say that a semisimplie Lie algebra g over Q has a simple factor of
type An if the base change g ⊗Q C has a simple factor of type An, and similarly
for the other simple types Bn, Cn, etc. We will require our varieties X to satisfy:
(∗) The LLV algebra llv(X; Q) does not have any simple factors of the type
C2, A3 or D4.


## Page 14


6
Introduction
Theorem 1.6. Let X and Y be smooth projective varieties over C satisfying
condition (∗). Assume that X and Y both admit a holomorphic symplectic form,
and assume that there exists an equivalence of triangulated categories Db(X) ≃
Db(Y ). Then there exists an isomorphism H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Y ; Q) respecting the
Hodge bigrading (i.e. there exists a degree-preserving isomorphism which, in each
degree, is an isomorphism of Hodge structures).
The proof of this theorem takes up most of the thesis; we finish the proof in
Chapter 6.
Remark 1.7. Condition (∗) suffices to prove Theorem 1.6, but in some cases
we can slightly weaken this condition. See Chapter 7 for more details, where we
state Theorem 1.6 in its most general form in Theorem 7.21.
Before we continue, let us discuss condition (∗) a bit. Unfortunately, it is not
vacuous. To construct an example of a variety violating this condition, let A be
an abelian surface (in particular, A admits a symplectic form) and let S be a
K3 surface equipped with a symplectic automorphism σ of order 5 (such a K3
surface exists, see for instance [Muk88]). Choose an element a ∈A of order 5.
Let G = ⟨g⟩be the cyclic group of order 5 and let G act on S × A by letting
g(s, x) = (σ(s), x + a). Then G acts freely on S × A and preserves a symplectic
form, so X := (S × A)/G admits a holomorphic symplectic form. By [GS07,
Proposition 1.1], we have dim H2(S; Q)σ = 6, and we will see (Theorem 3.78)
that the LLV algebra of X = (S × A)/G is a product of two factors of type D4.
However, we are still able to prove Conjecture 1.1 for varieties of the above
type. In fact, by studying the geometry of four-dimensional varieties with holo-
morphic symplectic forms, we will see that we do not need condition (∗) in di-
mension 4:
Theorem 1.8 (Theorem 7.17). Let X and Y be two smooth projective varieties
of dimension 4 over C, both admitting a holomorphic symplectic form, and as-
sume that there is a triangulated equivalence Db(X) ≃Db(Y ). Then there is an
isomorphism H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Y ; Q) which preserves the Hodge bigrading.
The above example can be generalized to higher dimensions, for instance by
taking the product of (S×A)/G with another symplectic variety. Therefore there
are still varieties admitting a holomorphic symplectic form to which Theorem 1.6
and its generalizations do not apply.
We will see in Lemma 3.77 that condition (∗) can be restated in a more
explicit manner. Using the Beauville-Bogomolov decomposition, we will see in
Section 2.5 that the variety X can be written in the form X ∼= (X0×Qk
i=1 Xi)/G,
where X0 is an abelian variety, the Xi are hyperkähler varieties, and G is a finite
group acting freely on X0 × Qk
i=1 Xi. Since H1(X0 × Qk
i=1 Xi; Q) = H1(X0; Q),


## Page 15


7
we obtain a representation of G on H1(X0; Q). Furthermore, for every 1 ≤i ≤k,
there is a maximal subgroup Gi ⊆G such that the action of G on X0 × Q Xi
restricts to an action of Gi on Xi (see Section 2.5 for more details). Write IrrC(G)
for a set of representatives for the isomorphism classes of irreducible C-valued G-
representations. Then (∗) is equivalent to:
(∗∗) The action of G on X has the following properties:
(a) Every self-dual ρ ∈IrrC(G) with (V2 ρ)G = 0 that occurs in the rep-
resentation G ↷H1(X0; C) does not have multiplicity 3 or 4.
(b) Every self-dual ρ ∈IrrC(G) with (V2 ρ)G = C that occurs in the
representation G ↷H1(X0; C) does not have multiplicity 2.
(c) Every non-self-dual ρ ∈IrrC(G) that occurs in the representation of G
on H1(X0; C) has multiplicity not equal to 2.
(d) For every i with 1 ≤i ≤k, the dimension of H2(Xi; Q)Gi is 5 or at
least 7.
The dimension of H2(Xi; Q)Gi is at least 3 (see Lemma 3.61), so property (d)
means that this dimension is not allowed to be 3, 4 or 6.
Lastly, in Chapter 8 we will prove Orlov’s conjecture for varieties with a tor-
sion canonical bundle whose simply connected covering is a hyperkähler variety.
Such varieties are sometimes called Enriques manifolds, as they generalize
Enriques surfaces to higher dimensions.
Theorem 1.9 (Theorem 8.2). Let X and Y be two Enriques manifolds. Let
Φ: Db(X) ≃Db(Y ) be an equivalence of triangulated categories. Then for every i
there is an isomorphism Hi(X; Q) ∼= Hi(Y ; Q) that preserves the Hodge structure.
The proof of this theorem is independent of the theory developed in the earlier
chapters of this thesis, and instead builds directly on Theorem 1.5.
Having discussed this, we give a rough outline of the proof of Theorem 1.6.
First, we will compute the LLV algebras of X and Y .
Using the Beauville-
Bogomolov decomposition, we can write the LLV algebra of X as a product of
smaller Lie algebras. We will then explicitly compute these smaller factors by
using algebras with involutions.
This is done in Chapter 3, with the results
summarized in Theorem 3.78.
By Theorem 1.5, the equivalence Φ: Db(X) →Db(Y ) induces an isomorphism
of LLV algebras Φllv : llv(X; Q) →llv(Y ; Q). The complexification of the LLV
algebra of a symplectic variety X contains two important elements, denoted by
hX and h′
X, which measure the cohomological grading and Hodge grading of the
cohomology H•(X; Q). In Chapters 5 and 6 we will construct an automorphism

PRELIMINARIES
In this chapter we recall some definitions and results that will be used throughout
the thesis. Most of the material here is well-known, and we provide proofs where
no reference could be found. We also prove a few new results in this chapter
which will be used later on.
2.1
Lie algebras
2.1.1
Isomorphisms of semisimple Lie algebras
Let g = g1 ×g2 be a product of Lie algebras over a field F. Given representations
Vi of gi for i = 1, 2, we can form the external tensor product V = V1 ⊠V2. This is
a representation of g1 ×g2, with underlying space V1 ⊗V2, and action determined
by letting (X, Y ) ∈g1 × g2 act on v ⊗w ∈V1 ⊗V2 by
(X, Y ) · (v ⊗w) = Xv ⊗w + v ⊗Y w.
Recall that for a Lie algebra g over a field F, a representation V of g is called
absolutely irreducible if V ⊗F F is an irreducible representation of g ⊗F F.
The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem:
Theorem 2.1. Let g = g1 × · · · × gn and h = h1 × · · · × hn be products of finite-
dimensional simple Lie algebras over a subfield F of C. For each i, let Vi be a
representation of gi, and let Wi be a representation of hi. Let V = V1 ⊠· · · ⊠Vn
and W = W1 ⊠· · · ⊠Wn, and assume that V and W are absolutely irreducible.
Let ψ: g →h be an isomorphism of Lie algebras, and let φ: V →W be a linear
isomorphism which is compatible with ψ, in the sense that φ(Xv) = ψ(X)φ(v)
for all X ∈g and v ∈V . Then there exists a permutation σ ∈Sn such that:


## Page 18


10
Preliminaries
(1) The map ψ is a product of isomorphisms ψi : gi →hσ(i).
(2) The map φ is a tensor product of isomorphisms φi : Vi →Wσ(i), which are
compatible with the Lie algebra action under ψi.
The first statement of the theorem is known, and follows from [Kna02, The-
orem 1.54]. I could not find the second statement in the literature, so we prove
it below.
We need a small lemma before we prove the above theorem.
Lemma 2.2. Let g1 and g2 be semisimple Lie algebras over a field F of charac-
teristic 0. For i = 1, 2, let Vi and Wi be finite-dimensional representations of gi,
and assume that V1 and W1 are irreducible. Let V = V1 ⊠V2 and W = W1 ⊠W2.
If V ∼= W, then V1 ∼= W1 and V2 ∼= W2.
Proof. Denote the inclusion g1 →g1 × g2 by i1.
Then precomposition with
i1 turns V into a g1-representation. Explicitly, this representation is given by
X·(v1⊗v2) = Xv1⊗v2. This implies that V , as a g1-representation, is isomorphic
to dim(V2) copies of V1. Similarly, we see that W is isomorphic to dim(W2) copies
of W1. Hence V dim(V2)
1
∼= W dim(W2)
1
, and the irreducibility of V1 and W1 then
implies that V1 ∼= W1 and dim(V2) = dim(W2). By an analogous argument we see
that V dim(V1)
2
∼= W dim(W1)
2
as representations of g2. Since dim(V1) = dim(W1),
this implies V2 ∼= W2.
Remark 2.3. The assumption that V1 and W1 are irreducible is crucial. An
easy counterexample is given by taking V1 and W2 the trivial representation, and
V2 and W1 two copies of the trivial representation.
We can now prove Theorem 2.1.
Proof of Theorem 2.1. By [Kna02, Theorem 1.54], the minimal ideals of g are
exactly g1, . . . , gn, and the minimal ideals of h are exactly h1, . . . , hn. Since an
isomorphism sends minimal ideals to minimal ideals, we see that there must be
a σ ∈Sn and isomorphisms ψi : gi →hσ(i) such that ψ is the product of the ψi.
This proves the first part of the theorem.
For the second part, we use Lemma 2.2 and Schurs lemma. By reindexing
the hi, we may assume that σ = id. Precomposition with ψ turns W into a
g-representation, where the action is given by X · w := ψ(X)w for X ∈g and
w ∈W. We denote this representation by Wψ. The map φ: V →Wψ is an
isomorphism of g-representations, since φ(Xv) = ψ(X)φ(v) for all X ∈g and
v ∈V . Since W = W1 ⊠· · · ⊠Wn and ψ is a direct product of the maps ψi,
we see that Wψ = W1,ψ1 ⊠· · · ⊠Wn,ψn, where Wi,ψi is the representation of
gi with underlying vector space Wi, and where Xi ∈gi acts on wi ∈Wi via
Xi · wi := ψi(Xi)wi.


## Page 19


Lie algebras
11
Therefore we have an isomorphism V1 ⊠· · ·⊠Vn ∼= W1,ψ1 ⊠· · ·⊠Wn,ψn. Since
V and W are irreducible, all Vi and products V1⊠· · ·⊠Vi must also be irreducible,
and the same holds for the Wi,ψi and products W1,ψ1 ⊠· · · ⊠Wi,ψi. Therefore,
we can inductively apply Lemma 2.2 to conclude that there are isomorphisms of
gi-representations eφi : Vi →Wi,ψi for all i.
To finish the proof, it suffices to show that φ = λeφ1⊗· · ·⊗eφn for some λ ∈F ∗.
By Schurs lemma, using that V and W are absolutely irreducible, the collection
of isomorphisms V ∼= Wψ is isomorphic to F ∗. Since both φ and eφ1 ⊗· · ·⊗eφn are
such isomorphisms, we see that they must be a scalar multiple of each other.
2.1.2
The centroid of a simple Lie algebra
We now recall some facts about the centroid of a simple Lie algebra. Given a
Lie algebra g over a field k, it could be possible that g is actually defined over a
bigger field. For example, we can consider sl2(Q(i)) as a Lie algebra over Q, but
it can also be given the structure of a Lie algebra over Q(i). If g is a simple Lie
algebra, the centroid is a field; it turns out to be the biggest field over which g is
defined.
We follow the book by Jacobson [Jac62, Chapter X], but our definition of the
centroid is slightly different from the one given there. The definition given in
[Jac62] works for arbitrary (non-associative) algebras, while the definition that
we give is specific to Lie algebras.
Definition 2.4. Let k be a field and let g be a Lie algebra over k. The centroid
of g, denoted by Z(g), is the endomorphism algebra of the adjoint representation
ad: g →gl(g). Explicitly,
Z(g) = {α ∈End(g) : ∀X ∈g, ad(X) ◦α = α ◦ad(X)}.
The proof of the following proposition is basically the same as given in [Jac62,
Chapter X, Lemma 1], but adapted to our definition of the centroid.
Proposition 2.5. Let g be a Lie algebra over a field k, and assume that ad: g →
gl(g) is injective. Then the centroid of g is a commutative k-algebra.
Proof. We claim that for all α ∈Z(g) and X ∈g, we have ad(X)◦α = ad(α(X)).
To see why, note that the equality ad(X)◦α = α◦ad(X) means that for all Y ∈g
we have
[X, α(Y )] = α([X, Y ]).
It follows that also [α(X), Y ] = α([X, Y ]) for all X, Y ∈g, so indeed ad(X) ◦α =
ad(α(X)) for all X ∈g.
If we now have α, β ∈Z(g), the above claim gives for all X ∈g:
ad(αβ(X)) = α ad(β(X)) = α ad(X)β = ad(α(X))β = ad(βα(X)).


## Page 20


12
Preliminaries
The injectivity of ad then implies that αβ(X) = βα(X) for all X ∈g, so indeed
αβ = βα.
Lemma 2.6. Let g be a simple Lie algebra over a field k. Then Z(g) is a field.
Proof. Since g is simple, the adjoint representation is a faithful irreducible rep-
resentation. Then Schurs lemma implies that Z(g) is a division algebra. It is
commutative by Proposition 2.5, so Z(g) is a field.
In particular, g is a Lie algebra over the field Z(g). The following result is a
variant of [Jac62, Chapter X, Theorem 3]:
Lemma 2.7. Let g be a finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra over a field k.
Then k = Z(g) if and only if g ⊗k k is simple.
Proof. We will first show that g ⊗Z(g) Z(g) is simple. We know that g ⊗Z(g) Z(g)
is semisimple, so it is a product of simple Lie algebras. The endomorphisms of
the adjoint representation of g ⊗Z(g) Z(g) are given by the base change of the
endomorphisms of the adjoint representation of g, so the endomorphism algebra
of the adjoint representation of g⊗Z(g) Z(g) is Z(g). This is a field, so the adjoint
representation of g ⊗Z(g) Z(g) is irreducible, which implies that g ⊗Z(g) Z(g) is a
simple Lie algebra.
Now assume that g ⊗k k is simple. For every λ ∈k, multiplication by λ is
an endomorphism of the adjoint representation, so k ⊆Z(g). Since g has finite
dimension over k, the extension Z(g)/k is finite, so k = Z(g). Therefore, we
have a surjective homomorphism of Lie algebras g ⊗k k →g ⊗Z(g) Z(g) given by
X ⊗α 7→αX. If k ̸= Z(g), this homomorphism is not injective for dimension
reasons, which implies that g ⊗k k is not simple.
Let k0 be the ground field of Z(g). If g is finite-dimensional over k0, then the
condition that k = Z(g) is automatically satisfied for any field k over which g is
defined.
Definition 2.8. Let k be a field and let g be a finite-dimensional simple Lie
algebra over k. If g ⊗Z(g) k ∼= sln+1(k), we say that g is of type An. We use the
analogous terminology for the other simple types, e.g. Bn, Cn and Dn.
Equivalently, g is of type An if g ⊗k k ∼= Qd
i=1 sln+1(k) for some integer d,
and we have similar statements for the other simple types.
As an example, consider the Lie algebra sl2(Q(i)) seen as a Lie algebra over
Q.
This is a simple Lie algebra of type A1, and we have sl2(Q(i)) ⊗Q Q ∼=
sl2(Q) × sl2(Q).

2.2
Algebras with involutions
In this section, we recall some properties of algebras with involutions. We will
see in Chapter 3 how these can be used to describe the LLV algebra of varieties
admitting a holomorphic symplectic form. The exposition here follows the Book
of Involutions [KMRT98], and we also mention some results from Jacobson [Jac62,
Chapter X].
Throughout this section, k will denote a field.
2.2.1
Definition and examples
Definition 2.9. Let A be an (associative, unital) algebra over k. An involution
on A is a map σ: A →A which is linear, anti-commutative (i.e. σ(ab) = σ(b)σ(a)
for a, b ∈A) and satisfies σ2 = id. We call the pair (A, σ) an algebra with
involution.
An involution is not required to be k-linear. For example, conjugation is an
involution of the C-algebra C.
Example 2.10. Let B be a k-algebra. Then A = B × Bop has an involution σ
defined by σ(b1, b2) = (b2, b1). The center of A is equal to Z(B) × Z(B), and σ
acts on this by swapping the two factors.
The following result can be found in [KMRT98, Chapter 1]:
Lemma 2.11. Let V be a vector space over k, equipped with a nondegenerate
bilinear form b that is either symmetric or anti-symmetric. Then for every a ∈
Endk(V ) there is a unique endomorphism σb(a) ∈Endk(V ) such that for all
v, w ∈V we have
b(av, w) = b(v, σb(a)w).
The obtained map σb : Endk(V ) →Endk(V ) is an involution.
We denote the obtained algebra with involution by (Endk(V ), σb).
Definition 2.12. Let (A, σ) be a k-algebra with involution, and let V be an
A-module. We say that a bilinear form b: V × V →k is (A, σ)-equivariant if,
for all a ∈A and v, w ∈V we have
b(av, w) = b(v, σ(a)w).
Remark 2.13. If the involution σ is clear from the context, we write A-equivariant
instead of (A, σ)-equivariant.


## Page 22


14
Preliminaries
Lemma 2.14. Let (A, σ) be an algebra with involution and let V be an A-
module with equivariant nondegenerate bilinear form b. Then the involution σb
on Endk(V ) restricts to an involution on EndA(V ).
We denote the obtained involution on EndA(V ) by eσ.
Proof. An f ∈Endk(V ) is in EndA(V ) if and only if f ◦a = a ◦f for all a ∈A.
Therefore we want to check that σb(f)◦a = a◦σb(f) for such f. If f ∈EndA(V )
and a ∈A, we see for all x, y ∈V that:
b(aσb(f)(x), y) = b(σb(f)(x), σ(a)y)
= b(x, f(σ(a)y))
= b(x, σ(a)f(y))
= b(ax, f(y))
= b(σb(f)(ax), y),
so we see that indeed σb(f) ∈EndA(V ).
Definition 2.15 (Dual of a module over an algebra with involution). Let (A, σ)
be an algebra with involution over k, and let V be an A-module. Then V ∗=
Hom(V, k) is a right A-module, and we turn this into a left A-module via the
isomorphism σ: A →Aop. Explicitly, for η ∈V ∗and a ∈A, we define aη ∈V ∗
by
(aη)(v) = η(σ(a)v)
for all v ∈V .
We will often be interested in bilinear forms arising in the following special
way. Let V be a vector space over a field k. We write eV = V ⊕V ∗. Then eV
carries a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form, given by
b((v, η), (v′, η′)) = η(v′) + η′(v),
where v, v′ ∈V and η, η′ ∈V ∗.
Lemma 2.16. Let (A, σ) be an algebra with involution over k and let V be an
A-module. Then the bilinear form b on eV = V ⊕V ∗defined above is (A, σ)-
equivariant.
The lemma can be proved by a direct computation, which we leave to the
reader.
By combining this with Lemma 2.14, we obtain an involution eσ on
EndA(eV ).


## Page 23


Algebras with involutions
15
2.2.2
Classification of involutions
Recall that a k-algebra A is central simple if and only if A⊗kk is of the form Mn(k)
for some integer n. If A is a finite-dimensional simple algebra, it is automatically
central simple over its center.
While the algebra A = B×Bop in Example 2.10 is not simple, it does not have
any nontrivial σ-invariant ideals if B is simple. Therefore, from the perspective
of algebras with involutions, it is simple in some sense.
Definition 2.17. Let A be a semisimple algebra over k and let σ be an involution
on A. If A has no nontrivial two-sided ideals I ⊆A with σ(I) = I, we say that
the pair (A, σ) is a simple algebra with involution.
Remark 2.18. It should be noted that a simple algebra with involution need
not be simple. The term should be read as: simple algebra-with-involution.
Definition 2.19. Let (A, σ) be a simple algebra with involution. We say that σ
is an involution of the first kind if σ|Z(A) = idZ(A). If σ does not act as the
identity on Z(A), we say that σ is an involution of the second kind.
Using the classification of semisimple algebras, one easily sees the following:
Lemma 2.20. Let (A, σ) be a simple algebra with involution.
1. If σ is an involution of the first kind, then Z(A) is a field.
2. If σ is an involution of the second kind, then ℓ:= Z(A)σ is a field, and
Z(A) is either a degree 2 field extension of ℓor Z(A) ∼= ℓ× ℓ, with σ|Z(A)
the swapping involution.
If Z(A) is a field, the underlying algebra A is simple, while A is a product of two
simple algebras if Z(A) is a product of two fields.
For example, if B is a simple k-algebra, the swapping involution on B × Bop
from Example 2.10 is an involution of the second kind. The involution constructed
in Lemma 2.11 is an involution of the first kind.
Lemma 2.21. Let (A, σ) be a finite-dimensional simple k-algebra with involution,
and let V be a nonzero finitely generated A-module equipped with a nondegenerate
symmetric or anti-symmetric A-equivariant bilinear form b. Then σ is an involu-
tion of the first kind if and only if the involution eσ on EndA(V ) is an involution
of the first kind.
Proof. We will first show that Z(A) = Z(EndA(V )). If Z(A) is a field, then A is
simple, and Wedderburn theory implies that Z(A) = Z(EndA(V )). Otherwise,
Z(A) is a product of two fields, and hence A is a product of two simple algebras


## Page 24


16
Preliminaries
and the involution σ is of the second kind. Then A has two simple modules up to
isomorphism, say V1 and V2, and these are dual to each other. We can then write
V ∼= V m
1
⊕V n
2 for some integers m and n. Since V carries a nondegenerate A-
equivariant bilinear form, we have an A-equivariant isomorphism V ∼= V ∗, which
implies that m = n. In particular, m and n are both nonzero, so we also have
Z(A) = Z(EndA(V )).
We will show that σ and eσ coincide on Z(A), so take a λ ∈Z(A). Since b is
A-equivariant, we have b(λv, w) = b(v, σ(λ)w) for all v, w ∈V . The involution eσ
is the adjoint map for b, so we also have b(λv, w) = b(v, eσ(λ)w) for all v, w ∈V .
Hence
b(v, σ(λ)w) = b(v, eσ(λ)w)
for all v, w ∈V . Since b is nondegenerate, it follows that σ(λ) = eσ(λ).
For simple algebras with involutions, we have the following classification the-
orem, see [KMRT98, Proposition 2.1 and Proposition 2.14]:
Theorem 2.22. Let k be a field of characteristic unequal to 2 and let (A, σ) be
a finite-dimensional simple k-algebra with involution.
(I) If the involution σ is of the first kind, then ℓ:= Z(A) is a field, and either
(1) there is an integer n ≥1 such that A ⊗ℓℓ∼= Mn(ℓ) and under this
isomorphism σ is given by the involution σb associated to some non-
degenerate symmetric bilinear form b on ℓ
n, or
(2) there is an even integer n ≥2 such that A ⊗ℓℓ∼= Mn(ℓ) and under
this isomorphism σ is given by the involution σb associated to some
nondegenerate skew-symmetric bilinear form b on ℓ
n.
(II) If the involution σ is of the second kind, then ℓ:= Z(A)σ is a field, and
Z(A) ⊗ℓℓ∼= ℓ× ℓ. Moreover:
(3) There is an integer n ≥1 such that A ⊗ℓℓ∼= Mn(ℓ) × Mn(ℓ)op, and
under this isomorphism σ is given by σ(x, y) = (y, x).
Using the classification theorem, we introduce a little bit more terminology:
Definition 2.23. Let (A, σ) be a simple algebra with involution.
1. If we land in the first case of the above theorem, we say that σ is an
orthogonal involution.
2. If we land in the second case of the above theorem, we say that σ is a
symplectic involution.


## Page 25


Algebras with involutions
17
3. If we land in the third case of the above theorem, we say that σ is a unitary
involution.
In particular, an involution is of the second kind if and only if it is unitary,
while an involution of the first kind is either orthogonal or symplectic.
2.2.3
The group of isometries
There are various groups that can be associated to an algebra with involution.
In this section, we will look at the group of isometries, and consider this as an
abstract group. Later, in Section 6.2.1, we will look at the group of isometries as
an algebraic group.
Definition 2.24. Let (A, σ) be an algebra with involution.
The group of
isometries of (A, σ) is defined by
Iso(A, σ) = {a ∈A× : aσ(a) = 1},
where A× ⊆A is the group of invertible elements.
Let V be an A-module, and suppose that V has an A-equivariant nondegener-
ate symmetric bilinear form b. Then we can look at the A-equivariant orthogonal
group, defined by
OA(V, b) = {f ∈O(V, b) : ∀a ∈A, v ∈V, f(av) = af(v)}.
Recall from Lemma 2.14 that EndA(V ) carries an involution eσ.
Lemma 2.25. There is an equality OA(V, b) = Iso(EndA(V ), eσ).
Proof. We have
EndA(V )× = {f ∈GL(V ) : ∀a ∈A, v ∈V, f(av) = af(v)}.
Both Iso(EndA(V ), eσ) and OA(V, b) are subgroups of EndA(V )×; it suffices to
show that for f ∈EndA(V )× we have f ∈O(V, b) if and only if f ◦eσ(f) = 1.
An endomorphism f of V is in O(V, b) if and only if for all v, w ∈V we have
b(f(v), f(w)) = b(v, w). But b(f(v), f(w)) = b(v, (eσ(f) ◦f)(w)). Therefore, we
have
b(v, w) = b(v, (eσ(f) ◦f)(w))
for all v, w ∈V . Since b is nondegenerate, this is equivalent to eσ(f) ◦f = idV ,
which is in turn equivalent to f ◦eσ(f) = idV .
Theorem 2.22 can be used to compute the group of isometries over an al-
gebraically closed field. The following result can be found in [KMRT98, Sec-
tion 23.A].


## Page 26


18
Preliminaries
Lemma 2.26. Let k be a field of characteristic unequal to 2, and let (A, σ) be a
finite-dimensional simple k-algebra with involution.
(I) If the involution σ is of the first kind with ℓ:= Z(A), let n be the integer
with A ⊗ℓℓ∼= Mn(ℓ).
(1) If σ is orthogonal, then Iso(A ⊗ℓℓ, σ) is isomorphic to the orthogonal
group O(n, ℓ).
(2) If σ is symplectic, then Iso(A ⊗ℓℓ, σ) is isomorphic to the symplectic
group Sp(n, ℓ).
(II) If the involution σ is of the second kind, let ℓ:= Z(A)σ and let n be the
integer such that A ⊗ℓℓ∼= Mn(ℓ) × Mn(ℓ).
(3) There is an isomorphism Iso(A ⊗ℓℓ, σ) ∼= GLn(ℓ).
Proof. This follows directly from Theorem 2.22.
2.2.4
Examples coming from representation theory
Representation theory gives useful examples of algebras with involutions. Indeed,
let k be a field and let G be a finite group. Then the group algebra
k[G] =



We now have two ways of taking the dual of a G-representation V . On the
one hand, we can take the dual as defined in representation theory, where we let
g ∈G act on η ∈V ∗by requiring that (gη)(v) = η(g−1v) for all v ∈V . On
the other hand, if we see V as a k[G]-module, we can take its dual using the
construction of Definition 2.15. It is easy to see that these two constructions
coincide:
Lemma 2.27. Let V be a G-representation.
Then the dual of V as a G-
representation coincides with the dual of V as a k[G]-module under the equiv-
alence of categories between G-representations and k[G]-modules.
Definition 2.28. Let G be a finite group and let k be a field. We write Irrk(G)
for a set of representatives of the irreducible k-valued G-representations.
If the characteristic of k does not divide |G|, the group algebra is semisimple,
so we can write k[G] = A1 × · · · × Ar for simple k-algebras A1, . . . , Ar. The

Therefore, the isomorphism classes of simple factors of k[G] are in bijection with
Irrk(G).
Lemma 2.29. Let G be a finite group, let k be a field of characteristic coprime to
|G|, let ρ ∈Irrk(G) and let A be the simple factor of k[G] associated to ρ. Then
the involution σ of k[G] restricts to an involution σ|A on A if and only if ρ is
self-dual. If ρ is not self-dual, the involution σ of k[G] restricts to the swapping
involution on A × A′, where A′ denotes the simple factor of k[G] associated to
ρ∗.
Proof. This follows directly from Lemma 2.27.

The above lemma implies the following:

coprime to |G|, let ρ ∈Irrk(G) be a self-dual irreducible representation and let
A be the simple factor of k[G] associated to ρ. Then σ|A is an involution of the
first kind if and only if all irreducible constituents of ρk are self-dual.
Proof. The base change Ak := A ⊗k k is a product of simple factors, say Ak ∼=

if it preserves these simple factors. And, as seen in Lemma 2.29, this happens if
and only if all irreducible constituents of ρk are self-dual.
Lemma 2.31. Let G be a finite group, let ρ ∈IrrQ(G) and let τ be an irreducible
constituent of ρC. Then τ is self-dual if and only if all irreducible constituents of

Proof. Decompose ρC = L ρni

Let χi be the character of ρi. Then the χi are all conjugate under the action of
Gal(Q/Q), see [Isa94, Theorem 9.21(c)]. Now ρi is self-dual if and only if χi = χi,

2.2.5
The Lie algebra of skew elements
We will now look at the collection of skew elements of an algebra with involution.
This is a Lie algebra, and will play an important role in understanding the LLV
algebra of a varieties admitting a holomorphic symplectic form.


## Page 28


20
Preliminaries
Definition 2.32. Let (A, σ) be a k-algebra with involution. The Lie algebra
of skew elements is the set
Skew(A, σ) := {a ∈A : σ(a) = −a},
equipped with the Lie bracket [a, b] = ab−ba. It is not hard to see that Skew(A, σ)
is indeed a Lie algebra (over the field kσ). We denote its derived Lie algebra by
s(A, σ), so
s(A, σ) = [Skew(A, σ), Skew(A, σ)].
In particular, we have a chain of inclusions
s(A, σ) ⊆Skew(A, σ) ⊆A.
For an algebra with involution of the form (EndA(V ), eσ), the Lie algebra
of skew elements has an alternative description in terms of the orthogonal or
symplectic Lie algebra, similar to Lemma 2.25:
Lemma 2.33. Let k be a field of characteristic not 2, let (A, σ) be an algebra with
involution over k and let V be an A-module equipped with an (A, σ)-equivariant
nondegenerate symmetric or skew-symmetric bilinear form b. Then
Skew(EndA(V ), eσ) =
(
so(V ) ∩EndA(V )
if b is symmetric
sp(V ) ∩EndA(V )
if b is skew-symmetric
as Lie subalgebras of gl(V ).
In particular, if A = k and σ = idk, then Skew(Endk(V ), σb) is either so(V, b)
or sp(V, b), depending on whether b is symmetric or skew-symmetric.
Proof. The proof is analogous to that of Lemma 2.25, where we now observe that
an f ∈Endk(V ) is in so(V ) if and only if b(f(v), w) + b(v, f(w)) = 0 for all
v, w ∈V , and that this is equivalent to eσ(f) + f = 0.
Let k be a field of characteristic not 2 and let (A, σ) be an algebra with
involution over k.
To understand s(A, σ), we first compute it after the base
change to k. The following theorem is a summary of some results that can be
found in [Jac62, Chapter X]:
Theorem 2.34. Let k be a field of characteristic not equal to 2 and let (A, σ) be
a finite-dimensional simple algebra with involution over k. Let n be the degree of
A over its center, so n2 = [A : Z(A)].
(I) If σ is an involution of the first kind, then ℓ:= Z(A) is a field and:
(1) If σ is symplectic, then n is even and s(A ⊗ℓℓ, σ) ∼= spn(ℓ).

(3) The involution σ is unitary, and s(A ⊗ℓℓ, σ) ∼= sln(ℓ).
In all cases above where s(A ⊗ℓℓ, σ) is simple, the centroid of s(A, σ) is equal to
ℓ= Z(A)σ.
In the terminology of Definition 2.8, we have the following:
1. If σ is symplectic, then s(A, σ) is of type Cl for some l ∈N. This is simple
if l ≥1.
2. If σ is orthogonal, then s(A, σ) is either of type Dl or type Bl for some
l ∈N. If it is of type Dl, it is simple for l ≥3, while it is simple for all
l ≥1 if it is of type Bl.
3. If σ is unitary, then s(A, σ) is of type Al−1 for some l. This is simple if
l ≥2.

2.2.6
Reconstructing an algebra with involution from its
Lie algebra of skew elements
An isomorphism (A, σ) →(B, τ) of algebras with involution induces an isomor-
phism between the Lie algebras s(A, σ) →s(B, τ). Conversely, a result by Jacob-
son says that, under suitable assumptions on A, every such isomorphism comes
from an isomorphism of algebras with involution. The following result is [Jac62,
Chapter X, Theorems 11 and 12]:
Theorem 2.36. Let k be a field of characteristic zero, and let (A, σ) and (B, τ)
be two finite-dimensional simple algebras with involution over k, and assume that
σ and τ restrict to the identity on k. Let ψ: s(A, σ) →s(B, τ) be an isomorphism
of Lie algebras over k, and assume that we are in one of the following cases:
1. σ and τ are unitary, and s(A, σ) is of type Al with l ≥2.
2. σ and τ are orthogonal, and s(A, σ) is of type Bl with l ≥2.
3. σ and τ are symplectic, and s(A, σ) is of type Cl with l ≥2.
4. σ and τ are orthogonal, and s(A, σ) is of type Dl with l = 3 or l ≥5.
Then ψ extends in a unique way to an isomorphism eψ: A →B of k-algebras,
satisfying τ ◦eψ = eψ ◦σ.
Proof. The proof is already given in the book of Jacobson, except for the cases
C2 and D3, and Jacobson does not prove that eψ commutes with the involutions.
Since the proof of the D3 case goes the same as that for Dl with l ≥5, we
give a uniform proof for both cases. We omit the proof of the C2 case, which is
analogous to the proof of the Cl case with l ≥3 as given in [Jac62].
So, assume that s(A, σ) is of type Dl, and let ψ: s(A, σ) →s(B, τ) be an
isomorphism.
Let ℓbe the center of A, and let ℓbe an algebraic closure of
ℓ. Then ℓis also the centroid of s(A, σ) by Theorem 2.34. Similarly, Z(B) is
isomorphic to the centroid of s(B, τ), so ψ induces an isomorphism ℓ→Z(B).

Remark 2.37. It seems to me that the cases of type C2 and D3 are excluded in
[Jac62, Chapter X] due to the exceptional isomorphisms B2 ∼= C2 and A3 ∼= D3.
Jacobson does not assume that σ and τ are both of the same type, and then such

This Lie algebra is independent of N, while the algebras A for different choices

The behaviour seen here can be explained by Galois cohomology. Let (A, σ)
be an algebra with involution, where σ is an involution of the second kind. If
s(A, σ) ⊗Q Q ∼= sl2(Q), then A ⊗Q Q ∼= M2(Q) × M2(Q)op, where σ becomes
the swapping involution τ. The collection of automorphisms of the algebra with
involution (M2(Q)×M2(Q)op, τ) is equal to PGL2(Q)⋊Z/2Z, so the twisted forms
of the algebra with involution (A, σ) are classified by the Galois cohomology set
H1(Q, PGL2(Q) ⋊Z/2Z).
On the other hand, the twisted forms of sl2(Q) are classified by the cohomology
set H1(Q, Aut(sl2(Q))), where Aut(sl2(Q)) ∼= PGL2(Q). Every automorphism
of (A, σ) induces an automorphism of s(A, σ), and this yields a surjective group
homomorphism
π: PGL2(Q) ⋊Z/2Z →PGL2(Q).

2.3
Algebraic groups
In this section we will discuss some results about algebraic groups that we will
need. We mostly follow the exposition from the Book of Involutions [KMRT98,
Chapter VI] and Milne [Mil11, Mil17]. Proposition 2.58 and Corollary 2.59 are
new results as far as I know.
Since we are mostly interested in affine algebraic groups, we will use the
following definition of an algebraic group:
Definition 2.38. Let k be a field. An algebraic group over k is an affine
group scheme G of finite type over k.
We will denote algebraic groups by an underline. For example, for a field k
and a k-vector space V , we let GL(V ) be the algebraic group whose R-points for
a commutative k-algebra R are given by GL(V ⊗k R).
2.3.1
Exactness in characteristic 0
Let k be a field, and let f : G →H be a morphism of algebraic groups over k.
Recall that the kernel of f is the algebraic group ker(f) whose R-points for a
commutative k-algebra R are given by
ker(f)(R) = ker(f : G(R) →H(R)).
If we write G = Spec(A) and H = Spec(B) with A and B Hopf algebras, then f
corresponds to a morphism of Hopf algebras f ∗: B →A. The image of f is the
algebraic subgroup of H corresponding to B/ ker(f ∗).

2.3.2
Weil restriction
Given a finite separable extension of fields F/k, we will often want to obtain an
algebraic group over k from an algebraic group defined over F. This can be done
by using Weil restriction.
Definition 2.40. Let F/k be a finite separable field extension and let G be an
algebraic group over F. The Weil restriction of G, denoted by RF/k G is the
algebraic group over k representing the functor A 7→G(A⊗kF) for a commutative
k-algebra A (this functor is representable by [KMRT98, Lemma 20.6]).
We will need a few properties of algebraic groups and Weil restriction.
Lemma 2.41. Let F/k be a finite separable field extension and let G be a smooth
algebraic group over F with Lie algebra g. Then the Lie algebra of RF/k G is
isomorphic to g seen as a Lie algebra over k (instead of a Lie algebra over F).
Proof. See [KMRT98, Proposition 21.4(3)].
Lemma 2.42 ([KMRT98, Proposition 20.7]). Let F/k be a finite separable field
extension of degree d, and let F be an algebraic closure of F. Then (RF/k G)(F) ∼=
(G(F))d.
Proof. This follows from the definition of Weil restriction. We have:
(RF/k G)(F) = G(F ⊗k F) ∼= (G(F))d.
Lemma 2.43. Let F/k be a finite extension of fields of characteristic 0 and let
1 →G1 →G2 →G3 →1 be a short exact sequence of algebraic groups over F.
Then the induced sequence
1
RF/k G1
RF/k G2
RF/k G3
1
is also exact.
Proof. Since k and F have characteristic 0, the result follows from Lemmas 2.39
and 2.42.


## Page 36


28
Preliminaries
Lemma 2.44. Let F/k be a finite separable field extension and let G and H be
algebraic groups over F. Then there is a canonical isomorphism RF/k(G × H) ∼=
RF/k G × RF/k H.
Proof. Weil restriction is right adjoint to the functor of extending scalars from k
to F [KMRT98, Proposition 20.7], so it preserves limits.
2.3.3
From Lie algebras to algebraic groups
A lot of the theory of algebraic groups is already captured by their Lie algebras,
especially over fields of characteristic 0. We will recall here a few results that we
will need about this.
Lemma 2.45 ([Mil11, Proposition II.2.13]). Let G and H be algebraic groups
over a field of characteristic 0, and let f, g: G →H be two homomorphisms. If
G is connected and the induced maps Lie(f), Lie(g): Lie(G) →Lie(H) coincide,
then f = g.
Lie’s third theorem says that every complex Lie algebra is the Lie algebra of a
complex Lie group. There is an analog for semisimple Lie algebras and algebraic
groups over fields other than the complex numbers. Before we state this result,
we need a few definitions. Recall that an algebraic group G over a field k is called
a group of multiplicative type if Gksep is isomorphic to a product of groups of the
form Gm,ksep and µn,ksep.
Definition 2.46. Let G and G′ be connected algebraic groups over a field k. A
homomorphism f : G′ →G is called a multiplicative isogeny if f is surjective
and ker(f) is a finite algebraic group of multiplicative type.
Definition 2.47. Let G be a connected semisimple algebraic group over a field k.
Then G is called simply connected if for every connected semisimple algebraic
group G′ and multiplicative isogeny f : G′ →G, the map f is an isomorphism.
The following is a useful way to prove that an algebraic group is simply
connected:
Lemma 2.48. Let G be a semisimple geometrically connected algebraic group
over a subfield k ⊆C. If G(C) is simply connected in the analytic topology, then
G is simply connected as an algebraic group over k.
Proof. Suppose G is not simply connected as an algebraic group. Then there
is a nontrivial multiplicative isogeny G′ →G. This gives a nontrivial covering
G′(C) →G(C), so G(C) is also not simply connected, which gives a contradiction.


## Page 37


Algebraic groups
29
Definition 2.49. Let k be a field.
• Let G be an algebraic group over k.
We denote its category of finite-
dimensional k-representations by Rep(G).
• Let g be a Lie algebra over k. We denote its category of finite-dimensional
k-representations by Rep(g).
With these definitions, we can state the analogue of Lie’s third theorem for
algebraic groups. The result is mostly proved in a book by Milne [Mil11].
Theorem 2.50. Let G be a semisimple simply connected algebraic group over a
field k of characteristic 0, and let g = Lie(G). Then taking Lie algebras gives an
equivalence of categories
Rep(G)
∼
→Rep(g).
Proof. By [Mil11, Theorem III.3.12] and [Mil11, Lemma III.3.8], there exists a
semisimple algebraic group G′ with Lie(G′) = g and an equivalence Rep(G′) ∼=
Rep(g). Moreover, part (c) of [Mil11, Theorem III.3.12] gives an isogeny f : G′ →
G. Since G′ is semisimple and k has characteristic zero, the kernel of f is of
multiplicative type. Since G is simply connected, f must be an isomorphism,
and this finishes the proof.
2.3.4
Cohomology of algebraic groups
Let G be an algebraic group over a field k, and let ℓ/k be a Galois extension.
Then Gal(ℓ/k) acts on G(ℓ). We denote the associated Galois cohomology by
Hi(ℓ/k, G) := Hi(Gal(ℓ/k), G(ℓ)).
If ℓ= ksep is the separable closure of k, we write Hi(k, G) := Hi(ksep/k, G). If G
is not abelian, the Galois cohomology is only defined for i ≤1.
We will also need the following result, which generalizes Hilbert 90:
Lemma 2.51. Let F/k be a finite separable field extension. Then
H1(k, RF/k Gm,F ) = 0.
Proof. Shapiro’s Lemma [KMRT98, Lemma 29.6] says that
H1(k, RF/k Gm,F ) ∼= H1(F, Gm,F ).
The result then follows from Hilbert 90.

Representation theory of some groups of multiplica-
tive type
We recall some facts about the representation theory of groups of multiplicative
type. Using this, we will describe a way of extending representations of algebraic
groups with a central factor Rℓ/k µn,ℓfor ℓ/k a finite seperable field extension,
see Corollary 2.59.
Definition 2.52. Let G be an algebraic group over a field k. The character
group of G is the abelian group
X(G) := Hom(Gksep, Gm,ksep).
The group Gal(ksep/k) acts on Gksep and Gm,ksep, and this induces an action
of Gal(ksep/k) on X(G), explicitly given by γ · f = γ ◦f ◦γ−1 for f ∈X(G)
and γ ∈Gal(ksep/k). We denote the set of orbits of the action of Gal(ksep/k) on
X(G) by X(G)/ Gal(ksep/k).
We will need a few facts about groups of multiplicative type. Their behaviour
is governed by their character groups:
Theorem 2.53. Let k be a field. Then taking the character group induces an
equivalence of categories between the category of groups of multiplicative type over
k and the category of finitely-generated abelian groups with a continuous action
of Gal(ksep/k).
Proof. See [Mil17, Theorem 12.23].
Lemma 2.54. Let k be a field and let G be a group of multiplicative type
over k.
Let Irrk(G) be a set of representatives for the isomorphism classes
of finite-dimensional irreducible G-representations over k.

of finite-dimensional G-representations is semisimple and there is a bijection
Irrk(G) →X(G)/ Gal(ksep/k), given by sending V ∈Irrk(G) to the collection
of characters occurring in Vksep.
Proof. See [Mil17, Theorem 12.30].

and Rℓ/k µn,ℓare groups of multiplicative type.

Let W be a vector space of dimension n over a field k. There is an inclusion
SL(W) →GL(W) of algebraic groups over k. Given a representation V of SL(W),
one may wonder whether it can be extended to a representation of GL(W). One
way to achieve this is to use that GL(W) is isomorphic to the quotient (Gm,k ×
SL(W))/µn,k, so we have to find a representation of Gm,k on V that commutes
with the SL(W)-representation such that the subgroup µn,k ⊆Gm,k × SL(V )
acts trivially on W.
Similar extension problems can be phrased in the same
way, for example for the inclusions Sp(V ) ⊆GSp(V ) for V a symplectic space
or Spin(V ) ⊆GSpin(V ) for V a quadratic space.
We will generalize this in
Corollary 2.59.
For an algebraic group G over a field k and a representation ρ: G →GL(V )
on a k-vector space V , we write CGL(V )(G) for the centralizer of ρ(G) in GL(V ),
this is an algebraic subgroup of GL(V ).

and R Gm in GL(V ) are equal.

2.4
Equivariant Hodge structures
In this section, we look at Hodge structures equipped with an action of a finite
group. I could not find a reference for the result of Proposition 2.61, so a proof
is given here.
Definition 2.60. Let G be a finite group.
A polarizable G-equivariant
Hodge structure is a polarizable rational Hodge stucture V which is also a
G-representation, such that for every g ∈G, the map g: V →V is a morphism
of Hodge structures.
If V and W are two G-equivariant Hodge structures, we say that a linear map
f : V →W is a morphism of G-equivariant Hodge structures if f is both
a morphism of Hodge structures and a morphism of G-representations.
These objects and morphism form the category of polarizable G-equivariant
Hodge structures.
Proposition 2.61. Let G be a finite group. Then:
1. The category of polarizable G-equivariant Hodge structures is semisimple.
2. Let V be a polarizable G-equivariant Hodge structure of weight k. Then
there is a G-equivariant isomorphism of Hodge structures V ∼= V ∗(−k).
Proof. Let V be a polarizable G-equivariant Hodge structure, and for every k ∈Z,
let Vk ⊆V be the subspace of elements of weight k. Then V ∼= L
k∈Z Vk, and also
gVk ⊆Vk for every g ∈G and k ∈Z. Hence it suffices to show that every k the
category of polarizable G-equivariant Hodge structures of weight k is semisimple.

For the second statement, observe that Q′ is a G-equivariant perfect pairing

2.5
The Beauville-Bogomolov decomposition
In this section we discuss the Beauville-Bogomolov decomposition, which can be
used to reduce the proof of Theorem 1.6 to the case where X and Y have the
specific form X = (X0 × Qk
i=1 Xi)/G and Y = (Y0 × Ql
j=1 ×Yj)/H, where the
Xi and Yj for i, j ≥1 are hyperkähler varieties, X0 and Y0 are even-dimensional
abelian varieties, and G and H are finite groups that act freely and preserve
at least one holomorphic symplectic form. After that, we will mention a few
constraints on the possible group actions on varieties of the form X0 × · · · × Xk
where X0 is an abelian variety and the Xi for i ≥1 are hyperkähler varieties. All
results in this section are due to Beauville [Bea83a].
Theorem 2.62. Let X be a smooth connected projective variety over C ad-
mitting a holomorphic symplectic form. Then there exist hyperkähler varieties
X1, . . . , Xk, an even-dimensional abelian variety X0 and a finite group G acting
freely on X0 × · · · × Xk, such that X ∼= (Qk
i=0 Xi)/G.
Proof. Since X is symplectic, it has trivial canonical bundle. Hence it admits an
étale covering A′ × Q X′
i × Q Yj →X where A′ is an abelian variety, the X′
i are
hyperkähler varieties and the Yj are strict Calabi-Yau varieties [Bea83a]. Since
the pullback of a symplectic form along an étale morphism is a symplectic form,
the étale covering is also symplectic. Hence we do not have any strict Calabi-Yau
factors and A′ has even dimension. The étale covering A′ × Q X′
i →X can be
refined to a Galois covering X0 × Qk
i=1 Xi →X with Galois group G (see the
proof of [Bea83a, Proposition 3]), so that X ∼= (X0 × Qk
i=1 Xi)/G. The action of
G on X is free because the map X →X/G is étale.

CHAPTER

THE LLV LIE ALGEBRA OF A VARIETY
ADMITTING A HOLOMORPHIC SYMPLECTIC
FORM
In this chapter, we will recall the definition of the LLV algebra of a projective
variety, due to Looijenga, Lunts and Verbitsky [LL97, Ver96a]. We then exten-
sively study the theory of LLV algebras. Some of the results presented here are
due to Looijenga and Lunts or Verbitsky, but this chapter also contains many
new results. This study of LLV algebras culminates in the computation of the
LLV algebra of a smooth projective variety admitting a holomorphic symplectic
form. Throughout this chapter, K will be a field of characteristic 0. All vector
spaces and algebras over K will be assumed to be finite-dimensional.
3.1
LLV algebras of (Jordan-)Lefschetz modules
In this section we recall the definition of Lefschetz modules (a, M) and their LLV
algebras, as introduced by Looijenga, Lunts and Verbitsky [LL97, Ver96a]. We
then study some of their properties.
Definition 3.1. Let M be a graded vector space of finite dimension over a field
K. We say that a linear endomorphism e: M →M of degree 2 has the hard
Lefschetz property if for every k ∈N the k-fold composite ek : M−k →Mk is
an isomorphism.
Let h: M →M be the linear endomorphism which acts as multiplication by k
on Mk. Given a degree 2 operator e: M →M with the hard Lefschetz property,
the Jacobson-Morozov theorem [Bou75, VIII.11.2, Proposition 2] says that there


## Page 46


38
The LLV Lie algebra of a variety admitting a holomorphic symplectic
form
is a unique endomorphism f : M →M of degree −2 giving an isomorphism
⟨e, h, f⟩∼= sl2(K), where e, h and f map to the standard generators ( 0 1
0 0 ),
  1
0
0 −1

and ( 0 0
1 0 ) of sl2(K).
In general, we can consider a K-vector space a and a map e: a →End(M)2
whose image consists of mutually commuting linear endomorphisms of degree
2.
We will assume that at least one element of the image of e has the hard
Lefschetz property. Since being invertible is a Zariski-open condition, a Zariski-
open collection of elements of im(e) has the hard Lefschetz property. For a ∈a,
we will denote the associated endomorphism of M by ea, and denote the ‘dual’
operator by fa (if it exists).
Definition 3.2. Let a be a K-vector space, M a graded K-vector space and
e: a →End(M)2 a linear map whose image consists of mutually commuting
endomorphisms of M. The LLV algebra of (a, M), denoted by llv(a, M), is the
Lie subalgebra of gl(M) generated by all Lie subalgebras sl2(K) ∼= ⟨ea, h, fa⟩⊆
gl(M) arising from a ∈a for which ea has the hard Lefschetz property.
Definition 3.3. Let a be a K-vector space, M a graded K-vector space and
e: a →End(M)2 a linear map whose image consists of mutually commuting
endomorphisms of M. We say that (a, M) is a Lefschetz module if the Lie
algebra llv(a, M) is semisimple.
For a graded vector space M and n ∈Z, we will write M[n] for M with
the grading shifted by n, so M[n]k = Mn+k. The motivating example of LLV
algebras comes from the cohomology of compact Kähler manifolds.
Example 3.4. Let X be a compact connected Kähler manifold of complex di-
mension n, let M = H•(X; Q)[n] and let a = H2(X; Q). Then a acts on M by
the cup product, and this indeed gives a collection of mutually commuting de-
gree 2 endomorphisms of M. We denote llv(a, M) by llv(X; Q). This Lie algebra
is nonzero, since any Kähler class has the hard Lefschetz property. Moreover,
llv(X; Q) is semisimple by [LL97, (1.9)], so (a, M) is a Lefschetz module.
We will also be interested in llv(X; K) for other fields K of characteristic 0.
Since Q ⊆K, there is still a Kähler class in H2(X; K), so llv(X; K) is still a
nonzero semisimple Lie algebra over K. There is a natural map llv(X; Q)⊗QK →
llv(X; K), and it is not hard to see that this map is an isomorphism. Therefore
it suffices to know llv(X; Q).
If M is a graded K-vector space, then EndK(M) obtains a grading, the degree
n part is equal to:

## Page 47


Tensor products and the LLV algebra
39
Then llv(a, M) inherits a grading via the inclusion llv(a, M) ⊆EndK(M).
The LLV algebras of the varieties that interest us in this thesis behave quite
nicely, they are all so-called Jordan-Lefschetz algebras. They have been studied
quite extensively by Looijenga–Lunts. For the proof that the properties in the
following definition are indeed equivalent, see [LL97, Proposition 2.1].
Definition 3.5. Let (a, M) be a Lefschetz module.
We say that (a, M) is a
Jordan-Lefschetz pair if the following equivalent properties hold:
1. llv(a, M) only lives in degrees −2, 0 and 2.
2. For any a, b ∈a that both have the hard Lefschetz property, the operators
fa and fb commute.
Looijenga and Lunts have classified all possible Jordan-Lefschetz pairs over
an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero [LL97, Corollary 2.6].
A first property of Jordan-Lefschetz pairs is that injectivity of morphisms
from their LLV algebras can be checked by only looking in degree 2.
Lemma 3.6. Let (a, M) be a Jordan-Lefschetz pair and let g = llv(a, M). Sup-
pose that h is a graded Lie algebra and that φ: g →h is a degree-preserving
homomorphism. If the restriction φ2 : g2 →h2 to degree 2 parts is injective, then
φ is injective.
Proof. Since g is semisimple, we can write g = Q
i gi as a product of simple Lie
algebras.
We start by observing that every gi is graded.
Indeed, every gi is
an g-representation and h is a semisimple element of g, so gi is the sum of its
eigenspaces for the action of h. Hence gk = L
i gi
k for k = −2, 0, 2.
Write g′ for the product of those gi which have a nonzero component in degree
2. By [Tae23, Proposition 2.1], any factor gi that does not contain an element
of degree 2 also does not contain any elements of degree −2, so the only way in
which φ can fail to be injective is if there is a simple factor living purely in degree
0. Hence, we are done if we show that g = g′.
By assumption, g′ contains all the ea with a ∈a. Since it is an ideal of g, it
also contains h = [ea, fa]. But then also all fa lie in g′ by [Tae23, Proposition 2.1].
Hence all generators of g lie in g′, so g = g′.
3.2
Tensor products and the LLV algebra
In this section we discuss the LLV algebra of a tensor product of Lefschetz mod-
ules.
Given two graded vector spaces M1 and M2 with vector spaces a1 and a2
acting on M1 and M2 respectively (with mutually commuting actions by degree


## Page 48


40
The LLV Lie algebra of a variety admitting a holomorphic symplectic
form
2 endomorphisms), we obtain an action of a1 ⊕a2 on the tensor product M1 ⊗M2
given by
(a1, a2) · (m1 ⊗m2) = a1m1 ⊗m2 + m1 ⊗a2m2.
This is also an action by mutually commuting degree 2 endomorphisms.
We equip gl(M1) × gl(M2) with a grading by setting
(gl(M1) × gl(M2))k = gl(M1)k × gl(M2)k,
and then we obtain a degree-preserving inclusion gl(M1)×gl(M2) →gl(M1⊗M2),
where we let (α, β) ∈gl(M1) × gl(M2) act on M1 ⊗M2 by sending m1 ⊗m2 to
α(m1) ⊗m2 + m1 ⊗β(m2). In the discussion above Lemma 1.2 in the paper
by Looijenga and Lunts [LL97], it is observed that the LLV algebra of such a
product is just the product of the LLV algebras:
Lemma 3.7. In the above situation, if M1 and M2 are nonzero and the actions
of a1 and a2 on M1 and M2 respectively have the hard Lefschetz property, then
the action of a1 ⊕a2 on M1 ⊗M2 also has the hard Lefschetz property. Moreover,
the natural degree-preserving inclusion gl(M1) × gl(M2) →gl(M1 ⊗M2) induces
an isomorphism llv(a1, M1) × llv(a2, M2) ∼= llv(a1 ⊕a2, M1 ⊗M2) of graded Lie
algebras.
It is not difficult to see that the tensor product of Jordan-Lefschetz pairs is
again a Jordan-Lefschetz pair:
Corollary 3.8. Let (a1, M1) and (a2, M2) be two Jordan-Lefschetz pairs. Then
(a1 ⊕a2, M1 ⊗M2) is also a Jordan-Lefschetz pair.
Proof. Let llvi = llv(ai, Mi) for i = 1, 2. Then llv1 and llv2 are both semisimple,
so llv1 × llv2 is also semisimple. Furthermore, if we denote by llvi,k the degree k
part of llvi, then (llv1 × llv2)k = llv1,k × llv2,k, so it immediately follows that the
pair (a1 ⊕a2, M1 ⊗M2) is Jordan-Lefschetz.
3.3
LLV algebras of graded commutative algebras
We will now turn our attention to Lefschetz pairs coming from a graded commuta-
tive (unital, associative) finite-dimensional K-algebra R, where we can define the
so-called Verbitsky component. We will see later that, under some assumptions,
the LLV algebra can be computed from just the Verbitsky component (Proposi-
tion 3.24). Recall that a graded K-algebra R is called graded commutative if, for
all integers n, m and elements a ∈Rn and b ∈Rm we have ab = (−1)mnba.
Definition 3.9. Let R be a graded K-algebra. If there is an integer n such that
R is supported in degrees 0 up to 2n and R2n ̸= 0, we say that R has depth n.


## Page 49


LLV algebras of graded commutative algebras
41
One could also wish to define the depth of a ring R supported in degrees 0 up
to 2n to be 2n, but we stick with this convention as has been set by Looijenga-
Lunts.
Definition 3.10. Let R be a graded commutative K-algebra of depth n. We say
that R has the hard Lefschetz property if at least one a ∈R2 has the hard
Lefschetz property for the action on R[n].
Example 3.11. Let X be a compact connected Kähler manifold of dimension n
over C. Then H•(X; K) is a graded commutative K-algebra of depth n. More-
over, the hard Lefschetz theorem says that the Kähler class in H2(X; Q) ⊆
H2(X; K) has the hard Lefschetz property.
Definition 3.12. Let R be a graded commutative K-algebra of depth n with
the hard Lefschetz property. Let a = R2 and M = R[n], where a ∈a acts on
R[n] by multiplication. We denote the resulting Lie algebra llv(a, M) by llv(R),
and call this the LLV algebra of R.
Recall that we require that the action of a on M is by mutually commuting
endomorphisms. This is indeed the case in the above, since R is graded commu-
tative.
Example 3.13. Continuing Example 3.11, let X be a compact connected Kähler
manifold of dimension n over C and let R = H•(X; K). Then the LLV algebra
llv(R) equals the Lie algebra llv(X; K) from Example 3.4.
Lemma 3.14. Let R be a graded commutative K-algebra of depth n with the
hard Lefschetz property. Then there is an injective map R2 →llv(R)2 sending
r ∈R2 to the operator er : R →R of multiplication by r.
Proof. Let U ⊆R2 be the set of r ∈R2 with the hard Lefschetz property. Then
U is Zariski-open in R2, so it is dense since K is infinite (recall that we assume
K to be of characteristic 0). Hence the map e: U →llv(R)2 which sends r to er
extends to all of R2 by linearity. If r, s ∈R2 and r ̸= s, then er ̸= es, so the map
e is injective.
One could wonder whether the natural map R2 →llv(R)2 also has to be
surjective. This is not the case, it is possible to construct examples where this
map is not surjective.
Lemma 3.15. Let R be a graded commutative K-algebra with the hard Lefschetz
property and let S ⊆R be a subalgebra of R. Assume that R and S both have
depth n, and assume there is an a ∈S2 ⊆R2 with the hard Lefschetz property
for both R[n] and S[n]. Write ea : R →R for the operator of multiplication by a,
and fa : R →R for its dual operator (so (ea, h, fa) is an sl2-triple). Similarly,
write (e′
a, h, f ′
a) for the sl2-triple associated to e′
a : S →S. Then f ′
a = fa|S.

42
The LLV Lie algebra of a variety admitting a holomorphic symplectic
form
Proof. This can be seen using the structure of irreducible sl2-representations. The
representation S[n] of sl2 = ⟨e′
a, h, f ′
a⟩splits as a direct sum L
i Vi of irreducible
representations Vi. Each such representation Vi can be understood as having a
piece of dimension 1 in degrees −k, −k + 2, . . . , k −2, k, where e′
a : Vi →Vi raises
the degree of each element by 2 and f ′
a lowers the degree by 2. Then [e′
a, f ′
a] = h.
Since ea|S = e′
a and Vi is generated by repeatedly applying e′
a to an element
of lowest degree, we see that the Vi ⊆R are also irreducible representations of
sl2 = ⟨ea, h, fa⟩. Hence, we see that fa|Vi = f ′
a|Vi for each i, which implies that
f ′
a = fa|S.
Corollary 3.16. Let R be a graded commutative K-algebra with the hard Lef-
schetz property and let S ⊆R be a subalgebra of R. Assume that R and S both
have depth n, and assume that there is an a ∈S2 ⊆R2 with the hard Lefschetz
property for R[n]. Then the following are equivalent:
1. The action of a on S[n] has the hard Lefschetz property.
2. The subspace S[n] ⊆R[n] is an invariant subspace for ⟨ea, h, fa⟩.
Proof. If the action of a on S[n] has the hard Lefschetz property, then Lemma 3.15
implies that the sl2-triple of the action of a on R[n] gives the sl2-triple for the
action of a on S[n], so S[n] is indeed an invariant subspace.
Now suppose that S[n] is an invariant subspace for ⟨ea, h, fa⟩.
Then S[n]
is a representation of sl2(K). The symmetry of sl2-representations implies that
dim Sn−k = dim Sn+k for all 0 < k ≤n. Since S ⊆R and R has the hard Lef-
schetz property, we know that ak : Rn−k →Rn+k is an isomorphism for all k, so
it induces injections Sn−k →Sn+k, which must be isomorphisms by the equality
of dimensions. Hence the action of a on S has the hard Lefschetz property.
Definition 3.17. Let R be a graded commutative K-algebra with the hard
Lefschetz property, and assume that R0 = K. The Verbitsky component of
R is the subalgebra SH(R) of R generated by R2.
Lemma 3.18. Let R be a graded commutative K-algebra with R0 = K and depth
n, and assume that R has the hard Lefschetz property. Then SH(R) also has depth
n.
Proof. Take an a ∈R2 with the hard Lefschetz property. Then R2n = K · an, so
an ̸= 0. But an ∈SH(R), so SH(R) also has depth n.
By applying Corollary 3.16 to SH(R), we obtain the following result. The fact
that the first statement implies the second is also proven by Verbitsky [Ver95,
Proposition 8.4].


## Page 51


LLV algebras of graded commutative algebras
43
Lemma 3.19. Let R be a graded commutative K-algebra with R0 = K and
depth n, and assume that R has the hard Lefschetz property. The following are
equivalent:
1. The K-algebra SH(R) has the hard Lefschetz property.
2. The subspace SH(R)[n] ⊆R[n] is llv(R)-invariant.
Proof. If SH(R) has the hard Lefschetz property, then Corollary 3.16 implies that
it is preserved by the action of all sl2-triples. Therefore it is closed under the
action of all generators of llv(R).
Conversely, if SH(R)[n] is llv(R)-invariant, it is in particular invariant under
the action of all sl2-triples associated to elements with the hard Lefschetz property
on R. Hence Corollary 3.16 implies that SH(R) has the hard Lefschetz property.
The following notions were introduced by Looijenga and Lunts and by Ver-
bitsky [LL97, Ver95].
Definition 3.20. Let R be a graded commutative K-algebra. We say that R is
Lefschetz (of depth n) if R has the following properties:
1. R0 = K.
2. R has depth n.
3. R has the hard Lefschetz property.
4. The LLV algebra llv(R) is a semisimple Lie algebra over K.
If (R2, R[n]) is in addition a Jordan-Lefschetz pair, we say that the algebra R is
Jordan-Lefschetz (of depth n).
Example 3.21. Let X be a compact connected Kähler manifold of dimension n
over C. Then H•(X; K) is Lefschetz of depth n, this follows from Example 3.4.
Moreover, it turns out that H•(X; Q) is Jordan-Lefschetz if X is a hyperkähler
or abelian variety (this was proven by both Looijenga-Lunts and Verbitsky for
hyperkähler varieties [LL97, Ver95], and by Looijenga-Lunts for abelian varieties
[LL97, Proposition 3.2]).
Lemma 3.22. Let R be a graded commutative Lefschetz algebra of depth n and
assume that SH(R) also has the hard Lefschetz property. Then SH(R)[n] is an
irreducible representation of llv(R).


## Page 52


44
The LLV Lie algebra of a variety admitting a holomorphic symplectic
form
Proof. The submodule SH(R)[n] ⊆R[n] has the hard Lefschetz property, so it is
a representation of llv(R) by Lemma 3.19.
Next, observe that any subrepresentation V ⊆R[n] is graded, in the sense
that if v ∈V can be written as v = P
m vm with vm ∈R[n]m, we have vm ∈V
for each m. This follows from the fact that the grading operator h ∈llv(R) is
semisimple, so its action on V is diagonalizable.
Since R0 = K, there must be a unique irreducible subrepresentation of R[n]
containing R0. Since R is Lefschetz, the Lie algebra llv(R) semisimple, so the
representation SH(R)[n] is completely reducible.
If we have a decomposition
SH(R)[n] = U ⊕V , then without loss of generality U−n ̸= 0 and V−n = 0. But
since SH(R) is generated by 1 and R2, we see that SH(R)[n] ⊆U, so SH(R)[n] is
irreducible.
Lemma 3.23. Let R be a graded commutative Lefschetz algebra of depth n and
assume that SH(R)[n] is an irreducible representation of llv(R). Then SH(R)[n]
is absolutely irreducible.
Proof. Since R0 = K, we also have SH(R)0 = K. Let A be the ring of endomor-
phisms of the llv(R)-representation SH(R)[n], then Schurs lemma implies that A
is a division algebra over K. Now take a ∈A and x ∈SH(R)0, and let h ∈llv(R)
be the grading operator. Then
h(ax) = ahx = −nax,
so also ax ∈SH(R)0. It follows that SH(R)0 = K is an A-module. Since A is
a division algebra over K, this is only possible if A = K. This implies that the
endomorphism algebra of SH(R)[n] ⊗K K is K, so Schurs lemma implies that
SH(R)[n] ⊗K K is also irreducible.
Let R be a graded commutative Jordan-Lefschetz algebra and assume that
SH(R) has the hard Lefschetz property. Since SH(R)2 = R2, any element of
R2 that has the hard Lefschetz property for R also has it for SH(R), so by
Lemma 3.15 we have a restriction morphism llv(R) →llv(SH(R)). The existence
of such a morphism was also shown by Verbitsky [Ver95, Corollary 8.2].
In
the next proposition, we will see that under suitable hypotheses this map is an
isomorphism, and we will later use this to compute some LLV algebras.
Proposition 3.24. Let R be a graded commutative Jordan-Lefschetz algebra.
Assume that the natural map R2 →llv(R)2 from Lemma 3.14 is bijective and that
SH(R) has the hard Lefschetz property. Then SH(R) is also Jordan-Lefschetz, and
the natural map llv(R) →llv(SH(R)) is an isomorphism.
Proof. Since SH(R)2 = R2, the natural map llv(R) →llv(SH(R)) is surjective.
Because R is Jordan-Lefschetz, its LLV algebra only lives only in degrees −2, 0

and 2, so the same holds for llv(SH(R)), which implies that SH(R) is Jordan-
Lefschetz as well.
Since SH(R) has the hard Lefschetz property, Lemma 3.14 gives an injection
R2 →llv(SH(R))2. The assumption that llv(R)2 = R2 then implies that the
map llv(R)2 →llv(SH(R))2 is injective. Then Lemma 3.6 implies that the map
llv(R) →llv(SH(R)) is injective. Since it is also surjective, we see that it must
be an isomorphism.
We end this section with the following result, which will be useful in combi-
nation with Lemma 3.7 to decompose the LLV algebra of a tensor product into
smaller factors.
Lemma 3.25. Let R and S be two graded commutative K-algebras of depth m
and n respectively. Let (r, s) ∈R2 ⊕S2 be an element whose action on R ⊗S has
the hard Lefschetz property. Then the actions of r on R and s on S both have
the hard Lefschetz property.
Proof. Consider the subalgebra
R(s) := R ⊗K[s]/(sn+1) ⊆R ⊗S.
The operator e(r,s) : R ⊗S →R ⊗S restricts to a map e(r,s) : R(s) →R(s). Since
e(r,s) has the hard Lefschetz property on R ⊗S, the map
ei
(r,s) : R(s)m+n−i →R(s)m+n+i
must be injective for all i, so dim Rm−i ≤dim Rm+i for all i. By switching the
roles of R and S, we see that also dim Sn−i ≤dim Sn+i for all i. Since R ⊗S has
the hard Lefschetz property, this is only possible if dim Rm−i = dim Rm+i and
dim Sn−i = dim Sn+i for all i.
The fact that the map ei
(r,s) : R(s)m+n−i →R(s)m+n+i is injective for all i
forces the map ei
r : Rm−i →Rm+i to also be injective for all i. The equality of
dimensions then implies that r has the hard Lefschetz property for its action on
R. Similarly, the action of s on S has the hard Lefschetz property.
Corollary 3.26. Let R and S be two graded commutative K-algebras of depth
m and n respectively, with R0 = K and S0 = K. Assume that R1 = 0, and
that R ⊗S has the hard Lefschetz property. Then R and S both have the hard
Lefschetz property.
Proof. Since R1 = 0 and R0 = S0 = K, we have (R ⊗S)2 = R2 ⊕S2. Therefore
there is an element (r, s) ∈R2 ⊕S2 = (R ⊗S)2 with the hard Lefschetz property,
and the result follows from Lemma 3.25.

46

3.4
LLV algebras of type A1
Let K be a field, and let R be a graded commutative Jordan-Lefschetz algebra
over K. Then llv(R) is semisimple, so it is a direct product of simple Lie algebras
over K. Let g be such a simple factor, and assume that g is of type A1. Recall
from Definition 2.8 that this means that there is a finite field extension F/K such
that g is defined over F, and g ⊗F F ∼= sl2(F). In this section we will show that
g is then actually isomorphic to sl2(F), so it cannot be a nontrivial twisted form
of sl2(F).
Lemma 3.27. Let K be a field of characteristic 0, let R be a graded commutative
Jordan-Lefschetz algebra over K, and let g be a simple factor of llv(R) of type
A1. Then g ∼= sl2(F), where F is the centroid of g.
Proof. As argued in the proof of Lemma 3.6, the simple factor g of llv(R) is a
graded Lie algebra, with grading induced by the grading on llv(R). Moreover, g
can not live purely in degree 0. In particular, there is a nonzero element e ∈g2.
Then e is a nilpotent element of g, since it acts nilpotently on R. Since F
is the centroid of g, we can see g as a Lie algebra over F.
By applying the
Jacobson-Morozov theorem over F to the element e ∈g, we obtain an injective
homomorphism j : sl2(F) →g, see [Bou75, VIII.11.2, Proposition 2]. Since g is
of type A1 and has centroid F, it has dimension 3 over F, so j is an isomorphism
sl2(F) ∼= g.

Lemma 3.34. Let R and S be graded commutative algebras of depth n and m,
both with the hard Lefschetz property. Let G be a group acting on R and S by
algebra automorphisms preserving the grading. Assume that (R1 ⊗S1)G = 0, that
