3.6
LLV algebras of compact Kähler manifolds
We will now study the LLV algebras of compact connected Kähler manifolds. Re-
call from Example 3.4 that for a compact connected Kähler manifold X, its LLV
algebra llv(X; K) is defined as llv(H2(X; K), H•(X; K)). We will write SH(X; K)
for the Verbitsky component SH(H•(X; K)), which is the subalgebra of H•(X; K)
generated by H2(X; K). We will now discuss the LLV algebras of hyperkähler
manifolds and abelian varieties, these are two fundamental examples for the rest
of this thesis. By using the decomposition from Theorem 2.62, we will reduce
the computation of the LLV algebra of a holomorphic symplectic variety to these
two cases.
Definition 3.35. Let A be an abelian variety, and let V = H1(A; K).
Let
eV = V ⊕V ∗, equipped with the bilinear form b given by
b((v, η), (v′, η′)) = η(v′) + η′(v).


## Page 57


LLV algebras of compact Kähler manifolds
49
We write Hev(A; K) = L
i H2i(A; K) for the even part of the cohomology
of A. In the next result, we use the spinor representation of an orthogonal Lie
algebra. See [FH91, Chapter 20] for more details on spinor representations.
Proposition 3.36. Let A be an abelian variety over C, and let V = H1(A; K).
Then there is an isomorphism
llv(A; K) ∼= so(eV , b).
Moreover, the Verbitsky component of A is equal to Hev(A; K), and is isomorphic
to the even spinor representation of so(eV , b).
Proof. See [LL97, Proposition 3.3], where this is proven with real coefficients.
Their proof also works with an arbitrary field of characteristic 0 as coefficients.
Definition 3.37. Let Y be a hyperkähler variety. The Mukai lattice of Y is
defined by
eH(Y ; K) = Kα ⊕H2(Y ; K) ⊕Kβ.
We equip eH(Y ; K) with a bilinear form b by taking the orthogonal sum of the
Beauville-Bogomolov-Fujiki form on H2(Y ; K) and the hyperbolic plane Kα⊕Kβ
(where b(α, β) = −1). The grading on the Mukai lattice is given by giving α
degree −2, giving β degree 2 and by giving H2(Y ; K) degree 0.
Proposition 3.38. Let Y be a hyperkähler variety. Then there is an isomor-

pullback π∗ω is a Kähler class on X. Hence π∗ω has the hard Lefschetz property
for both H•(X; Q) and H•(X; Q)G.

We will now restrict ourselves to smooth projective varieties over C which can

3.8
Decomposing the LLV algebra of X
In this section we split the LLV algebra of a variety X = (Q Xi)/G as in
Setup 3.43 into a direct product of smaller factors, and in Sections 3.9 and 3.10
we will explicitly compute these factors. Recall that K denotes a field of charac-
teristic 0.
Lemma 3.49. Let X be as in Setup 3.43. Then the actions of G on H•(X0; K)
and H•(XHK; K) satisfy the conditions of Setup 3.28, and the graded commutative
rings H•(X0; K)G and H•(XHK; K)G are Jordan-Lefschetz.
Proof. The action of G on H•(Qk
i=0 Xi; K) satisfies the conditions of Setup 3.28
by Lemma 3.40, and H•(Qk
i=0 Xi; K) is Jordan-Lefschetz by Proposition 3.45.
Lemma 3.34 implies that the actions of G on H•(X0; K) and H•(XHK; K) satisfy
the conditions of Setup 3.28. Then H•(X0; K)G and H•(XHK; K)G are Jordan-
Lefschetz by Lemma 3.32.
In particular, the rings H•(X0; K)G and H•(XHK; K)G have the hard Lef-
schetz property, so their LLV algebras are defined.
Definition 3.50. Let llvAb(X; K) = llv(H•(X0; K)G), we call this the abelian
part of the LLV algebra of X. Similarly, let llvHK(X; K) = llv(H•(XHK; K)G),
we call this the hyperkähler part of llv(X; K). We call the simple factors of
llvAb(X; K) the factors of abelian origin, and the simple factors of llvHK(X; K)
the factors of hyperkähler origin.
A priori, the Lie algebras llvAb(X; K) and llvHK(X; K) depend on the cho-
sen presentation of X as a quotient X = (Q Xi)/G. However, we will see in
Lemma 3.54 below that llvAb(X; K) and llvHK(X; K) do not depend on this pre-
sentation. Therefore the Definition 3.50 is independent of the chosen quotient
presentation of X.
Our aim in this section is to prove that llv(X; K) ∼= llvAb(X; K)×llvHK(X; K).
The inclusion
H•(X0; F)G ⊗H•(XHK; K)G ⊆H•(X; K)G

is a strict inclusion in general, so we cannot directly apply Lemma 3.7. However,
by looking at Verbitsky components, we can still compute llv(X; K). Therefore,
we introduce the following notation:

Definition 3.51. Let X be as in Setup 3.43. We write SHAb(X; K) for the
Verbitsky component of the ring H•(X0; K)G, and SHHK(X; K) for the Verbitsky
component of H•(XHK; K)G.
Proposition 3.52. Let X be as in Setup 3.43. Then the inclusion (3.1) induces
an isomorphism
SHAb(X; K) ⊗SHHK(X; K) ∼= SH(X; K).
Proof. The inclusion (3.1) restricts to an injective ring homomorphism
ι: SHAb(X; K) ⊗SHHK(X; K) →H•(X; K).
Since H1(XHK; K) = 0, we have H2(X; K) ∼= H2(X0; K)G ⊕H2(XHK; K)G, so ι
is an isomorphism in degree 2. Since the Verbitsky component is exactly the
subring generated by the elements in degree 2, it follows that the image of ι is
equal to SH(X; K).
Corollary 3.53. Let X be as in Setup 3.43. Then there is an isomorphism
llv(X; K) ∼= llvAb(X; K) × llvHK(X; K).
Proof. By Proposition 3.47, SH(X; K) has the hard Lefschetz property. By tak-
ing G-invariants in Lemma 3.46, we see that llv(X; K)2 = H2(X; K). Hence we
can apply Proposition 3.24 to conclude that llv(X; K) ∼= llv(SH(X; K)). Corol-
lary 3.26 implies that both SHAb(X; K) and SHHK(X; K) have the hard Lefschetz
property. Then Lemma 3.7 and Proposition 3.52 imply that
llv(X; K) ∼= llv(SHAb(X; K)) × llv(SHHK(X; K)).
Then Proposition 3.24 gives isomorphisms llv(SHAb(X; K)) ∼= llv(H•(X0; K)G)
and llv(SHHK(X; K)) ∼= llv(H•(XHK; K)G), which finishes the proof.

Lemma 3.59. Let g be a semisimple Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field.
Let h be a Cartan subalgebra of g, with associated root system R. Let R′ ⊆R
be a subset of roots with the property that every element of R is a finite sum of
elements of R′. Then the Lie algebra g is generated by the subset ⊕α∈R′gα.
Proof. Write g′ for the Lie subalgebra of g generated by ⊕α∈R′gα. We know that
g = h ⊕L
α∈R gα. For any two roots α and β of g with α + β also a root, the
inclusion [gα, gβ] ⊆gα+β is an equality, see [Bou75, VIII.2.4, Lemma 4]. Since
R′ additively generates R and all root spaces are one-dimensional, we see that
every root space gα for α ∈R is contained in g′.
It remains to show that h ⊆g′. The Cartan subalgebra is spanned by the
coroots tα for α ∈R, and for every root α and nonzero elements xα ∈gα
and x−α ∈g−α, the element [xα, x−α] is a nonzero multiple of tα by [Hum78,
Proposition 8.3]. Hence h ⊆g′.
The following lemma is proven by Verbitsky [Ver95, Theorem 9.1], but we
also give a self-contained proof here.

Recall from Definition 3.37 that the Mukai lattice eH(Y ; K) of a hyperkähler
variety Y is the direct sum of H2(Y ; K) and a hyperbolic plane U = Kα ⊕Kβ.
Then eH(Y ; K) is a non-degenerate graded quadratic space, with H2(Y ; K) in
degree 0. For a group H of automorphisms of Y , we define an action of H on
eH(Y ; K) by taking the direct sum of the induced action of H on H2(Y ; K) and
the trivial action of H on the hyperbolic plane U.
In Proposition 3.38 we saw that llv(Y ; K) ∼= so(eH(Y ; K)) for a hyperkähler

Recall that if we have a field F and a central simple algebra A over F, then
dimF (A) = d2 for some integer d, and we call d the degree of A. The degree is
invariant under base change, i.e. we have deg(A) = deg(A ⊗F L) for every field
extension L/F.
If (A, σ) is a simple algebra with involution which comes from an equivalence

Definition 3.70. Let (A, σ) be a simple algebra with involution, and assume
that A = A1 × A2 is a product of two simple algebras, with σ the swapping
involution. We define the degree of (A, σ) to be deg(A, σ) = deg(A1).
We will generally remove the σ from the notation, so we just write deg(A) for
deg(A, σ) if this causes no confusion. Note that the index is stable under base
change in the following sense: if (A, σ) is an algebra with involution of the second
kind, and F := Z(A) is a field, then for every field extension L of F σ, we have
deg(A) = deg(A ⊗F σ L),
where the index on the right hand side is understood to be deg(A ⊗F σ L, σ ⊗id)
if A ⊗F σ L is a product of two simple algebras.

Lemma 3.71. Let V, G and [ρ] be as in Setup 3.68, and let eA = EndA(eV ),
equipped with the involution eσ. Then deg( eA) is even, and s( eA, eσ) is a simple Lie

lemma about the Lie algebra of skew elements of a product of algebras with
involution. Given two algebras with involution (A, σ) and (B, τ), the product
A × B carries a natural involution σ × τ, given by (σ × τ)(a, b) = (σ(a), τ(b)).
Lemma 3.74. Let (A, σ) and (B, τ) be algebras with involution. Then
Skew(A × B, σ × τ) ∼= Skew(A, σ) × Skew(B, τ).
Proof. For a ∈A and b ∈B, the condition that (σ×τ)(a, b) = −(a, b) is equivalent
to σ(a) = −a and τ(b) = −b.
Let G be a finite group and let ρ ∈IrrQ(G). Choose an irreducible constituent
τ of ρC. Then we have already seen that τ is self-dual if and only if all irreducible
constituents of ρC are self-dual (Lemma 2.31). If τ is self-dual, then character
theory implies that (τ ⊗τ)G = C. Since V2 τ ⊆τ ⊗τ is a subrepresentation, one
may ask whether the trivial subrepresentation of τ ⊗τ already occurs in V2 τ.
Lemma 3.75. Let G be a finite group, let ρ ∈IrrQ(G) and let τ be an irreducible

Now that we have computed the LLV algebra more explicitly, we can also
prove the equivalence of the conditions (∗) and (∗∗) introduced in Chapter 1.
Lemma 3.77. Let X = (X0 × Qk
i=1 Xi)/G, where X0 is an abelian variety, the
Xi for i = 1, . . . , k are hyperkähler varieties and G is a finite group whose action
on Qk
i=0 Xi preserves a symplectic form. Then X satisfies condition (∗) if and
only if it satisfies condition (∗∗).
Proof. The equivalence of these two conditions follows from studying in which
ways different simple factors arise. Write llv(X; Q) = h1 × · · · × hm as a product
of simple Lie algebras.
Let h be one of the hα for 1 ≤α ≤m, and first assume that h ∼= so(eH(Xi; Q)Gi)
for some i. This is of type Bn or Dn. Since B2 ∼= C2 and D3 ∼= A3, we see that
condition (∗) says that h is of type Bn with n ≥3 or of type Dn with n ≥5. This
is equivalent to the requirement that dim H2(Xi; Q)Gi is 5 or at least 7, which is
exactly what is demanded by condition (∗∗).
Now suppose we are in the other case. Then there is a ρ ∈IrrQ(G) with asso-
ciated simple factor A of Q[G], and for V the ρ-isotypic component of H1(X0; Q)
we have an isomorphism h ∼= s(EndA(eV ), eσ) (unless h is of type A1). Then for
every irreducible constituent ρi of ρC, the multiplicity of ρi in H1(X0; C) is equal
to the integer l = deg( eA)/2 from Lemma 3.71. Hence h is of type Dl, Cl or A2l−1,
depending on whether eσ is orthogonal, symplectic or unitary. The equivalence of
conditions (∗) and (∗∗) now follows from Lemma 3.76.

Let X = (Q
i Xi)/G be as in Setup 3.43, and assume that X satisfies condition (∗).
Let K be a field of characteristic 0. In the previous chapter, we computed the

study these factors in more detail, and describe them as representations of the

We saw in Theorem 3.78 that the factors of SH(X; Q) are of two types, either

component V[ρ] of the G-representation H1(X0; Q). We will study the factors of

tion 4.2.

We will now compute the Verbitsky component SHG(V[ρ]; C) associated to simple
factors of llv(X; C) of the type llvG(V[ρ]; C). We will do this by computing the
highest weight of SHG(V[ρ]; C) (this is the reason why we work over C instead of
Q in this section). We will only need this computation in the cases where the Lie
algebra llvG(V[ρ]; C) is of type An or Dn for some n > 1, but the computation
for type Cn is similar and can also be performed. We start with some technical
preliminaries.

We also have the following result, whose second part generalizes [Tae23, The-
orem 4.4]:
Theorem 4.8. Let X and Y be smooth projective varieties over C carrying
a holomorphic symplectic form, and let Φ: Db(X) →Db(Y ) be a triangulated
equivalence. Then there is an isomorphism of Lie algebras Φllv : llv(X; Q) →
llv(Y ; Q) so that ΦH : H•(X; Q) →H•(Y ; Q) is equivariant with respect to Φllv.
Furthermore, the map ΦH restricts to an isomorphism of Verbitsky components
ΦSH : SH(X; Q) →SH(Y ; Q).
Proof. The existence of Φllv and the proof that ΦH is equivariant with respect
to Φllv is [Tae23, Theorem A]. To see that ΦH restricts to an isomorphism of
the Verbitsky components, we can use the same proof as [Tae23, Theorem 4.4].
Recall that SH(X; Q) is an irreducible llv(X; Q)-representation by Lemma 3.23.
Let d = dim(X), and let ω be a symplectic form on X. Then multiplication with
ωd/2 induces an isomorphism OX ∼= Ωd
X, so H0(X, Ωd
X) ∼= C. We have ωd/2 ∈
SH(X; Q), so the Verbitsky component is the unique irreducible subrepresentation
of H•(X; Q) that contains the classes of Hodge type (d, 0) and (0, d). Since ΦH
respects the columns of the Hodge diamond and is equivariant with respect to
Φllv, we see that it must send SH(X; Q) to SH(Y ; Q).

In this section, we use our computations of the LLV algebra and the derived
invariance of the LLV algebra to give an alternative proof of (a slight general-
ization of) Theorem 1.4, which was originally proven by Huybrechts and Nieper-
Wisskirchen. It should be noted that both their and our argument in the end boil
down to the derived invariance of Hochschild (co)homology. The argument by
Huybrechts and Nieper-Wisskirchen uses this rather directly, while in our proof
this fact is used by Taelman in the proof of Theorem 1.5.

dim Xi = dim Yσ(i) and b2(Xi) = b2(Yσ(i)) for all i.

i=0 Xi and Y = Qn

llv(Y ; Q), with the property that the isomorphism ΦH : H•(X; Q) →H•(Y ; Q) is
equivariant with respect to Φllv. Recall that there is an element hX ∈llv(X; Q)
which acts on Hi(X; Q) as multiplication by dim(X) −i, and there is an element
h′
X ∈llv(X; C) which acts on Hp,q(X) as multiplication by by q −p. Together,
the actions of hX and h′
X determine the Hodge bigrading on H•(X; Q) (we dis-
cuss h′
X in more detail in Section 5.2). The goal of this chapter is to prove the
following theorem:
Theorem 5.1. Let X and Y be smooth projective varieties over C, admitting a
holomorphic symplectic form and satisfying condition (∗), and let Φ: Db(X) →
Db(Y ) be an equivalence of triangulated categories. Then there exist:
1. A reductive algebraic group H over Q,
2. an inclusion llv(Y ; Q) ⊆Lie(H),
3. and an element ψ ∈H(Q),
satisfying the following properties:
1. The automorphism Ad(ψ) ∈Aut(Lie(H)) sends llv(Y ; Q) to itself.

h′
X to h′
Y .

We will prove this theorem by constructing H and ψ ‘factorwise’. In chapters 3
and 4, we computed the LLV algebras and Verbitsky components of X and Y , and
obtained an explicit description of the simple factors of llv(X; Q) and llv(Y ; Q).
Write llv(X; Q) = Q
α gα and llv(Y ; Q) = Q
β hβ for the decompositions into
simple factors obtained in Theorem 3.78. By Theorem 2.1, after reordering the
hβ, the isomorphism Φllv is a product of isomorphisms Φllv

We
will then construct for every α a reductive algebraic group Hα over Q with

α
gives an

The Lie algebras Lie(Hα) do not differ too much from the hα: for every α, the
Lie algebra Lie(Hα) will either be equal to hα, or it will be the direct product of
hα and an abelian Lie algebra. In particular, Lie(Hα) is a reductive Lie algebra
with semisimple part isomorphic to hα.
5.1
Reduction to simple factors
Recall from Theorem 3.78 that for a variety X = (Qk
i=0 Xi)/G as in Setup 3.43,
which additionally satisfies condition (∗), the simple factors g of the LLV algebra
can be of two possible forms. Either there is a hyperkähler factor Xi such that g ∼=
so(eH(Xi; Q)Gi), in which case we say that g is of hyperkähler origin. Otherwise,
there is an isotypic component Vρ ⊆H1(X0; Q) with associated simple algebra
A ⊆Q[G] such that g ∼= s(EndA(eVρ), eσ). In this case we say that g is of abelian
origin.
In Proposition 5.4 below, we will show that if the isomorphism Φllv
matches up two simple factors g and h of llv(X; Q) and llv(Y ; Q) respectively,
they must both be of hyperkähler origin or both of abelian origin. For this, we
need the following result about the signature of the Mukai lattice:
Lemma 5.2. Let Z be a hyperkähler variety and let G be a finite group of sym-
plectic automorphisms of Z. Then eH(Z; R)G has signature (4, k) for some k ∈N.
Proof. The quadratic space H2(Z; R)G has signature (3, l) for some l ∈N by
[Mon16, Lemma 24]. The result then follows from the fact that eH(Z; R)G is the
direct sum of a hyperbolic plane and H2(Z; R)G.
We will also need the following result.
It follows from [Con14, Proposi-
tion C.3.14], where it is proven in much more generality (including the case
n = 8). However, it is not difficult to give a proof using Theorem 2.36.

Lemma 5.3. Let (V, b) and (W, b′) be two quadratic spaces over a field k of
characteristic zero, and let φ: so(V, b) →so(W, b′) be an isomorphism of Lie
algebras. Let n = dimk(V ). If n ≥5 and n ̸= 8, then there is a scalar λ ∈k∗
such (V, b) ∼= (W, λb′) as quadratic spaces.
Proof. Let A = Endk(V ) and B = Endk(W), equipped with the adjoint invo-
lutions σ and τ corresponding to b and b′ respectively. By Theorem 2.36, the
map φ lifts to an involution-preserving isomorphism eφ: A
∼
→B (here we use the
assumptions on dimk(V )). The Skolem-Noether theorem implies that the isomor-
phism eφ is of the form eφ = Ad(f) for some isomorphism f : V
∼
→W. The result
now follows from the fact that, up to multiplication by a scalar, the bilinear form
can be recovered from its adjoint involution [KMRT98, Page 1].
Proposition 5.4. Let X and Y be smooth projective varieties over C, admitting
a holomorphic symplectic form and satisfying condition (∗). Let g and h be simple
factors of llv(X; Q) and llv(Y ; Q) respectively. Assume that g ∼= h and that g is
not of type A1.
1. If g is of hyperkähler origin, then so is h.
2. If g ∼= s(EndA(eV ), eσ) for an isotypic component V of H1(X0; Q), then
h ∼= s(EndB(f
W), eτ) for an isotypic component W of H1(Y0; Q), and the
involutions eσ and eτ are of the same type.
Proof. This follows from listing all possible isomorphism types of the simple fac-
tors of the LLV algebra.
If a simple factor of llv(X; Q) is of hyperkähler origin, it is isomorphic to
so(eH(Xj; Q)Gj) for some hyperkähler variety Xj. This Lie algebra is either of
type Dn or Bn for some n. We know from Lemma 3.61 that dim eH(Xj; Q)Gj ≥5.
If g is a factor of abelian origin, then we have seen that it can be of type
An, Cn or Dn, depending on the type of the involution. By condition (∗) and the
assumption that g is not of type A1, it cannot be of type A1, C2, A3 or D4 (and
hence also not of type D3, because A3 ∼= D3). We have also seen that type D2
does not occur, since in this case only one factor of type A1 ends up in the LLV
algebra (see Theorem 3.73). Similarly, since C1 ∼= A1, a simple factor of type Cn
must have n ≥3. Since this exhausts all exceptional isomorphisms between the
types An, Cn and Dn, we see that if g and h are two simple factors of abelian
origin, they must come from the same type of involution.
Up to symmetry, the only way in which an isomorphism g ∼= h can occur with
g and h of different origin is if g is of hyperkähler origin and h is of abelian origin,
and both are of type Dn for some n ≥5. We will show that such an isomorphism
cannot occur over R.

5.2
The Hodge operator
Recall that for a graded vector space V over a field k, we have a grading operator
h which, for every integer i, acts on the graded piece Vi as multiplication by i.
Similarly, if V is equipped with a Hodge structure, we have the Hodge operator h′:
Definition 5.6. Let V be a Q-Hodge structure. The Hodge operator h′ is the
linear endomorphism h′ of VC which for every pair of integers (p, q) acts as mul-
tiplication by (q −p) on V p,q.
The eigenspaces of h′ are the columns of the Hodge diamond of V . Let V and
W be two vector spaces over Q, both equipped with a pure Hodge structure, and
with Hodge operators h′
V and h′
W respectively. Then a linear map f : V →W
respects the Hodge grading if and only if f ◦h′
V = h′
W ◦f, and f respects the
grading if and only if f ◦hV = hW ◦f. Then f is a morphism of Hodge structures
if and only if f commutes with both h and h′. To stress this, we say that such
an f respects the Hodge bigrading.
Lemma 5.7 ([Tae23, Corollary 2.12]). Let X be a smooth projective variety over
C admitting a holomorphic symplectic form and let h′

Hodge operator of X decomposes as h′
X = (h′
X,α)α, where h′
X,α ∈gα,C := gα⊗QC
for all α.

X,α ∈gα,C
be the α-th component of the Hodge operator.
1. If gα ∼= so(eH(Xj; Q)Gj) is of hyperkähler origin, then h′
X,α as an endomor-
phism of eH(Xj; Q)Gj acts as the Hodge operator of eH(Xj; Q)Gj.

X,α acts on eV as the Hodge operator of eV .

Q

Proof of Theorem 5.1
In this section, we will prove Theorem 5.1. We will prove it factorwise, as ex-
plained in the introduction to this chapter. In Section 5.3.1, we will prove it for
factors of abelian origin, except for those of type A1. We prove it for factors of
type A1 in Section 5.3.2, and lastly we prove it for simple factors of hyperkähler
origin in Section 5.3.3. We then summarize these results in Section 5.3.4.

This isomorphism D ∼= E can be made explicit by taking a simple eA-module

The isomorphism eϕ: eA →eB preserves the involution.
Lemma 2.21 shows that the involutions on A and eA are of the same kind, and

Lemma 5.15. Assume that we are in the setting of Lemma 5.14. Furthermore,
assume that A is a simple factor of the group algebra Q[G] of some finite group
G. Then there exists an equivariant isometry eV →f
W that preserves the Hodge
bigrading. In particular, there exists an element ψ ∈Sim( eB, eτ) so that ψ ◦f
preserves the Hodge bigrading.
Proof. Since A is a simple factor of Q[G], there is a multiplicative map G →A. By
composing with the isomorphism β : A →B, we also obtain a multiplicative map
G →B, so W obtains the structure of a G-representation. Hence, V ⊕V ∗(−1)
and W ⊕W ∗(−1) are both objects of the category of polarizable G-equivariant
weight 1 Hodge structures. By Proposition 2.61, this category is semisimple, and
we have isomorphisms W ∼= W ∗(−1) and V ∼= V ∗(−1).
Since the isomorphism f : eV →f
W from Lemma 5.14 is equivariant and re-
spects the Hodge grading, it gives an isomorphism of equivariant weight 1 Hodge
structures V ⊕V ∗(−1) →W ⊕W ∗(−1). Therefore, as G-equivariant weight 1
Hodge structures, V and W must have the same simple factors with the same
multiplicities, so there exists a G-equivariant isomorphism of Hodge structures
g: V →W. Let g∗: W ∗→V ∗be the dual morphism of g, and let eg = g⊕(g∗)−1.
Then eg: eV →f
W is the desired morphism, and we can take ψ = eg ◦f −1. Then
ψ ◦f preserves the Hodge bigrading.
It remains to show that ψ ∈Sim( eB, eτ).
Since eg is an isometry, we have
Ad(eg) ◦eσ = eτ ◦Ad(eg). Therefore also Ad(ψ) ◦eτ = eτ ◦Ad(ψ), so for all b ∈eB we
have
ψ ◦eτ(b) ◦ψ−1 = eτ(ψ−1) ◦eτ(b) ◦eτ(ψ).
Since eτ : eB →eB is surjective, this implies that eτ(ψ)ψ commutes with all b ∈eB,
so eτ(ψ)ψ ∈Z( eB), and therefore ψ ∈Sim( eB, eτ).
Recall that the group of isometries of an algebra with involution (A, σ) is
defined by
Iso(A, σ) := {a ∈A× : aσ(a) = 1}.
Equivalently, one could define this as the set of similitudes a ∈Sim(A, σ) with
aσ(a) = 1. In particular, there is an inclusion Iso(A, σ) ⊆Sim(A, σ).

5.3.2
Factors of type A1
In this section, we prove Theorem 5.1 for simple factors of llv(X; Q) of type A1.
We will first see that the component of the Hodge operator in such a simple factor
vanishes. We then only have to deal with the grading, and this can be done with
some Lie theory.
Lemma 5.18. Let X be a smooth projective variety as in Setup 3.43, admitting
a holomorphic symplectic form, and let gα be a simple factor of llv(X; Q) of type
A1. Let h′ ∈gα be the component in gα of the Hodge operator of X. Then h′ = 0.
Proof. Write X = (Qk
i=0 Xi)/G, and let F be the centroid of gα. Then gα ∼=
sl2(F) by Lemma 3.27. Any simple factor of llv(X; Q) coming from a hyperkähler
factor Xi is of the form so(eH(Xi; Q)Gi), and the dimension of eH(Xi; Q)Gi is at
least 5 by Lemma 3.61. This implies that our simple factor gα must be of abelian
origin.
Theorem 3.78 implies that gα is of the form llvG(V ; Q) := llv((V• V )G; Q)
for some isotypic component V ⊆H1(X0; Q). Since X is projective, X0 is also
projective, and therefore H1(X0; Q) is a polarizable Hodge structure of weight
1. Since V ⊆H1(X0; Q) is a sub Hodge structure, it is polarizable as well. In
Proposition 2.61, we saw that V therefore has a G-equivariant polarization, and
this gives an algebraic element of (V2 V )G. In particular, we see that (V2 V )G ∩
(V2 V )1,1 ̸= 0. Let y ∈(V2 V )G ∩(V2 V )1,1 be such a nonzero element.


## Page 106


98
Construction of ψ
Let gα,2 be the degree 2 part of gα, then there is an isomorphism gα,2 ∼=
(V2 V )G which preserves the Hodge structure. Moreover, since gα is of type A1
with centroid F, every element x ∈gα,2 is of the form x = λy for some λ ∈F.
We now claim that for every λ ∈F, multiplication by λ as a map gα →gα
preserves the Hodge structure.
To see why, recall that gα is isomorphic to a
Lie subalgebra of so(eV ), see diagram (3.6) and Proposition 3.69. We know that
V ⊆H1(X0; Q) is an isotypic component. Let A be the simple factor of Q[G]
associated to this isotypic component. The action of G on V gives eV the structure
of an A-module, and through this EndQ(eV ) also becomes an A-module (defined
for f ∈EndQ(eV ) and a ∈A by (a · f)(v) = af(σ(a)v) for all v ∈eV , where σ is
the involution on A). Since the action of G on V preserves the Hodge structure,
the action of every a ∈A on V preserves the Hodge structure. In particular, the
action of F = Z(A)σ on EndQ(eV ) preserves the Hodge structure, and this implies
that for every λ ∈F, multiplication by λ as a map gα →gα preserves the Hodge
structure, which proves the claim.
It now follows that all elements of gα,2 are algebraic, and therefore all elements
of gα are algebraic, which implies that h′ = 0.
Lemma 5.19. Let F be a field of characteristic 0, let gα ∼= sl2(F) and let
⟨e1, h1, f1⟩and ⟨e2, h2, f2⟩be two sl2-triples in gα. Then the F-linear map gα →
gα which sends e1 to e2, sends h1 to h2 and sends f1 to f2 is a Lie algebra
automorphism of gα.
Proof. This follows immediately from the definition of an sl2-triple.
By using these two results, we can prove Theorem 5.1 for simple factors of
type A1.
Corollary 5.20. Let X and Y be two smooth projective varieties over C, sat-
isfying condition (∗). Let Φ: Db(X) →Db(Y ) be a triangulated equivalence, let
gα be a simple factor of llv(X; Q) of type A1 and let hα be the simple factor of
llv(Y ; Q) such that Φllv induces an isomorphism Φllv
α : gα →hα. Let F be the
centroid of gα. Then there is a ψα ∈GL2(F) such that Ad(ψα) ◦Φllv
α : gα →hα
preserves the Hodge bigrading.
Proof. First observe that hα ∼= sl2(F) by Lemma 3.27. Moreover, the Hodge
operators in gα and hα are 0 by Lemma 5.18.
Let eX ∈gα,2 be an element with the hard Lefschetz property, which gives

If H would be simply connected with Lie(H) = llv(Y ; Q), then we could

6.2.1
Algebraic groups associated to algebras with involu-
tions
Let (A, σ) be a simple algebra with involution over a field k and let g = s(A, σ).
Except for some low-degree exceptions, g is a semisimple Lie algebra over kσ,
see Theorem 2.34. Hence there exists a unique simply connected linear algebraic
group Γ over kσ with Lie(Γ) ∼= g. In this section, we will explicitly construct Γ.


## Page 116


108
Construction of ΨH
We will start with some generalities, following [KMRT98, Section 23]. Let
(A, σ) be a simple algebra with involution over a field F, and assume that the
center of A is either F (if (A, σ) is of the first kind) or an étale quadratic extension
Z(A)/F with F = Z(A)σ (if (A, σ) is of the second kind).
Associated to such a pair (A, σ), one can associate the group of isometries:
Definition 6.6. The algebraic group Iso(A, σ) of isometries of (A, σ) is the
algebraic group over F = Z(A)σ, whose R-points for an F-algebra R are given
by:
Iso(A, σ)(R) = {a ∈A×
R : aσ(a) = 1},
where AR = A ⊗F R.
Recall from Lemma 2.26 that the group of isometries over an algebraically
closed field is either the orthogonal group, the symplectic group or the general
linear group, depending on whether σ is orthogonal, symplectic or unitary.
The Lie algebra of the group of isometries is computed in [KMRT98, Sec-
tion 23.A]:
Lemma 6.7. Let (A, σ) be a simple algebra with involution, and let F = Z(A)σ.
Then there is an isomorphism Lie(Iso(A, σ)) ∼= Skew(A, σ) of Lie algebras over F.
This implies the following (recall that s(A, σ) is the derived Lie algebra of
Skew(A, σ)):
Corollary 6.8. Let (A, σ) be a finite-dimensional simple algebra with involution
over a field k of characteristic zero, and assume that k = kσ. Let F = Z(A)σ,
let the algebraic F-group Iso(A, σ)′ be the commutator subgroup of Iso(A, σ), and
let Γ = RF/k Iso(A, σ)′. Then there is an isomorphism
Lie(Γ) ∼= s(A, σ)
of Lie algebras over k, compatible with the isomorphism from Lemma 6.7.
Proof. By Lemma 6.7, there is an isomorphism Lie(Iso(A, σ)) ∼= Skew(A, σ) of
Lie algebras over F. Taking the commutator subgroup corresponds to taking
the derived Lie algebra, so this induces an isomorphism Lie(Iso(A, σ)′) ∼= s(A, σ)
of Lie algebras over F.
The field extension F/k is finite because A is finite-
dimensional over k, and it is separable because k has characteristic 0, so the
result now follows from Lemma 2.41.
6.2.2
Orthogonal involutions
In this section we partially prove Theorem 6.2 for hα ∼= s( eB, eτ) with eτ an orthog-
onal involution. Before we do this, we summarize some properties of spin groups
associated to algebras with orthogonal involutions.


## Page 117


Lifting for factors of abelian origin
109
Spin groups
Let A be a finite-dimensional central simple algebra over a field k of characteris-
tic 0, equipped with an orthogonal involution σ. We write O(A, σ) for Iso(A, σ),
this is an algebraic group over k. This algebraic group is disconnected, and we de-
note the connected component of the identity by SO(A, σ). Since every connected
variety with a rational point is geometrically connected [Sta25, Lemma 04KV],
we see that SO(A, σ)(k) ∼= SOn(k) for some integer n.
The group SO(A, σ) is not simply connected.
However, SO(A, σ) admits
a double covering by a simply connected algebraic group over k, denoted by
Spin(A, σ). Similar to the case of the classical spin group, there is also a version
of the general spin group, which we denote by GSpin(A, σ). We now summarize
the most important properties of Spin(A, σ) and GSpin(A, σ).
Definition 6.9. Let A be a central simple algebra over a field k of character-
istic 0, equipped with an orthogonal involution σ. We define Spin(A, σ) to be
the simply connected cover of SO(A, σ), and call Spin(A, σ) the spin group of
(A, σ). It is an algebraic group over k = Z(A).
Remark 6.10. For an explicit construction of Spin(A, σ) in terms of Clifford
algebras, see [KMRT98, Section 23.B].
The essential properties of Spin(A, σ) that we will need are summarized in the
following theorem. The following properties (and a lot more about spin groups)
are discussed in the Book of Involutions [KMRT98, Section 23.B].
Theorem 6.11. Let A be a central simple algebra over a field k of characteris-
tic 0, equipped with an orthogonal involution σ. Then there exists a short exact
sequence of algebraic groups over k
1 →µ2,k →Spin(A, σ) →SO(A, σ) →1.
Moreover, this short exact sequence has the following properties:
1. The formation of this short exact sequence commutes with base change.
2. If A ∼= Endk(V ) for a finite-dimensional k-vector space V , and V is equipped
with a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form b such that σ is the adjoint
involution of b, then the short exact sequence is isomorphic to the short
exact sequence
1 →µ2,k →Spin(V, b) →SO(V, b) →1.


## Page 118


110
Construction of ΨH
Now that we have the group Spin(A, σ), we can define GSpin(A, σ) as follows:
Definition 6.12. Let A be a central simple algebra over a field k of characteris-
tic 0, equipped with an orthogonal involution σ. Let GSpin(A, σ) be the algebraic
group over k defined by
GSpin(A, σ) := (Gm,k × Spin(A, σ))/µ2,k,
where the inclusion µ2 →Gm×Spin(A, σ) is the product of the standard inclusion
µ2 →Gm and the inclusion µ2 →Spin(A, σ) from the above theorem. We call
GSpin(A, σ) the general spin group of A.
Remark 6.13. The groups Spin(A, σ) and GSpin(A, σ) are algebraic groups
over the center of A. Often, we will consider A as an algebra over a field k, with
possibly a bigger center F = Z(A). In those cases, the group Spin(A, σ) is defined
over F, and if we want to obtain a group over k, we take the Weil restriction
RF/k Spin(A, σ).
The group GSpin(A, σ) comes with a map to SO(A, σ). Indeed, consider the
trivial map Gm →SO(A, σ) which sends everything to the identity, and the map
Spin(A, σ) →SO(A, σ). The product of these two maps contains µ2 in its kernel,
and hence induces a map GSpin(A, σ) →SO(A, σ).
The group GSpin(A, σ)
interacts just as expected with the groups SO(A, σ) and Spin(A, σ):
Lemma 6.14. Let (A, σ) be a finite-dimensional simple algebra with orthogonal
involution over a field k, and let F = Z(A). Then the group RF/k GSpin(A, σ)
fits in the short exact sequences:
1 →RF/k µ2,F →RF/k Gm,F × RF/k Spin(A, σ) →RF/k GSpin(A, σ) →1
and
1 →RF/k Gm,F →RF/k GSpin(A, σ) →RF/k SO(A, σ) →1.
Proof. Since Weil restriction commutes with products and preserves exactness

6.2.4
Unitary involutions
We will now treat the unitary case, so assume that hα ∼= s( eB, eτ) with eτ a unitary
involution, and assume that hα is not of type A1. Let K = Z( eB), so τ acts
nontrivially on K, and we have [K : Kτ] = 2. Define ΓHα = RK eτ /Q Iso( eB, eτ)
as an algebraic group over Q. We then have ψα ∈ΓHα(Q) for the element ψα
constructed in Corollary 5.17. The difficulty in proving Theorem 6.2 in this case
is the construction of a representation of ΓHα on H•(Y ; Q).
If eτ is unitary, recall from Lemma 2.26 that the algebraic group Iso( eB, eτ) is
a form of the general linear group GLn for some n ≥1. In particular, Iso( eB, eτ)
is reductive but not semisimple. Its Lie algebra is Skew( eB, eτ), and we have a
strict inclusion s( eB, eτ) ⊂Skew( eB, eτ). Only the Lie subalgebra s( eB, eτ) occurs
in the LLV algebra, so by integrating the action of the LLV algebra on the
cohomology we only get a representation of RK eτ /Q Iso( eB, eτ)′ on H•(Y ; Q) (note

The norm homomorphism
We will now discuss a generalization of the norm homomorphism to algebraic
groups, and study its kernel. For a detailed reference, see [Rui20].
For a finite field extension K/F, the norm homomorphism NK/F : K× →F ×
induces a homomorphism
NK/F : RK/F Gm,K →Gm,F
of algebraic groups over F; the induced map on F-points is precisely the norm
homomorphism NK/F : K× →F ×.
Definition 6.20. Let K/F be a finite separable field extension. We write SK/F
for the algebraic group over F defined by
SK/F := ker(NK/F : RK/F Gm,K →Gm,F ).
For t ∈N, we will also look at the subgroup SK/F [t] ⊆SK/F of t-torsion
elements. The norm homomorphism restricts to a homomorphism RK/F µt,K →
µt,F , and this gives an isomorphism
SK/F [t] ∼= ker(NK/F : RK/F µt,K →µt,F ).
During the rest of this section, the field K will be Z( eB), and we will always
take F = Kτ. For this setting, we will just write S instead of SK/Kτ , this is an
algebraic group over Kτ. We will denote the Weil restriction RKτ /Q S by R S, this
is an algebraic group over Q. For the extension K/Kτ, the norm homomorphism
is given by sending x ∈K× to xτ(x), so
S(Kτ) = {x ∈K× : xτ(x) = 1}.
This description of the norm homomorphism implies that we have a homo-
morphism S →Iso( eB, eτ). By computing the base change to K, we see that this
identifies S with the center of Iso( eB, eτ).
Recall that n is the integer such that eB ⊗Kτ K ∼= Mn(K) × Mn(K)op, and
that Iso( eB, eτ)′ denotes the commutator subgroup of Iso( eB, eτ).

1 →S[n] →S × Iso( eB, eτ)′ →Iso( eB, eτ) →1,

In the proof, we will also need the following notions:
Definition 6.43. Let k be a field, let g be a Lie algebra over k, let H be a
finite group and let V be an H-representation over k, and assume that V is also
a g-representation. We say that g acts H-equivariantly on V if for all h ∈H
and x ∈g the actions of h and x on V commute.
For a field k and a finite group H, if V is an H-representation over k, the
map ρ: H →GL(V ) induces a map ρ: H →GL(V ) of algebraic groups over k,
where H is seen as a constant algebraic group over k.
Definition 6.44. Let G be an algebraic group over a field k, let H be a finite
group, and let ρ: H →GL(V ) be an H-representation over k. Assume that V is
also a G-representation. We say that G acts H-equivariantly on V if the image
of G →GL(V ) is contained in the centralizer of ρ(H) ⊆GL(V ).

## Page 139


CHAPTER
SEVEN
GENERALIZATIONS OF THE MAIN THEOREM
During the proof of Theorem 1.6, we had to assume that the varieties X and
Y satisfy condition (∗).
In this chapter, we will discuss some ways in which
condition (∗) can be relaxed in Theorem 1.6.
7.1
Restrictions of simple factors
Let X be a smooth projective variety over C admitting a holomorphic symplectic
form. By Theorem 2.62, we can write X as a quotient X ∼= (Qk
i=0 Xi)/G, where
X0 is an abelian variety, and the Xi for i = 1, . . . , k are hyperkähler varieties.
Using this quotient presentation, we computed llv(X; Q) in Theorem 3.78, and
we saw that each simple factor of llv(X; Q) either comes from the abelian part
or the hyperkähler part. Moreover, according to Lemma 3.54, this distinction is
independent of the chosen quotient presentation.
Each simple factor of hyperkähler origin is of type Bn or Dn for some n ≥2,
while each simple factor of abelian origin is of type An, Cn or Dn for some n ≥1
(see Theorem 3.78). In Proposition 5.4 we saw that, if X satisfies condition (∗),
the isomorphism class of a simple factor of llv(X; Q) determines whether it is
of hyperkähler or of abelian origin.
However, this is not true if X does not
satisfy condition (∗) anymore. This is caused by the existence of some exceptional
isomorphisms of Lie algebras (notably, B2 ∼= C2 and A3 ∼= D3). For example, a
simple factor of type C2 does not necessarily have to be of abelian origin, it can
also be of hyperkähler origin.
This means that the proof of Proposition 5.4 breaks down without condi-
tion (∗). However, we can slightly weaken this condition by requiring that (some

The proof of Corollary 5.22 does not use condition (∗) in any way. This condition

2. In the proof of Corollary 5.17.

Theorem 7.1. Let X and Y be smooth projective varieties over C, both admitting
a holomorphic symplectic form.

Similar to the previous section, we can wonder what happens if we instead restrict
the possible simple factors of llv(X; Q) and llv(Y ; Q) of hyperkähler origin, and
loosen the restrictions on the simple factors of abelian origin. Here the answer
is unfortunately not as nice as above, but we can still make some progress. For
the three simple types of factors excluded by condition (∗), we now only exclude
type D4, and assume that the factors of type C2 and A3 are of abelian origin:
Theorem 7.2. Let X and Y be smooth projective varieties over C, both admitting
a holomorphic symplectic form. Moreover, assume that llv(X; Q) and llv(Y ; Q) do
not have any simple factors of type D4, and that all simple factors of types C2 and
A3 in llv(X; Q) and llv(Y ; Q) are of abelian origin. Let Φ: Db(X) ≃Db(Y ) be
a triangulated equivalence. Then there is an isomorphism H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Y ; Q)
which preserves the Hodge bigrading.
Before we prove this theorem, we need a small refinement of Proposition 5.4.
If gα is a simple factor of llv(X; Q) of type A3, there are three possible ways in
which gα could have been constructed (recall that D3 ∼= A3):

If we assume that X0 is symplectic and g is a symplectic automorphism, we
can use the isotypic decomposition to show something even stronger:
Corollary 7.12. Let X0 be an even-dimensional complex abelian variety, and
let g: X →X be an automorphism of finite order, preserving a holomorphic
symplectic form on X0. If g has no fixed points, then the automorphism g∗of
H1(X0; Q) has the eigenvalue 1 with multiplicity at least 4.
Proof. Let G = ⟨g⟩⊆Aut(X0) be the finite group generated by g.
Since
g∗: H1(X0; Q) →H1(X0; Q) has eigenvalue 1 with multiplicity at least 1, there
is a v ∈H1(X0; C) which is invariant under g, and hence under the action of
G = ⟨g⟩. It follows that the trivial representation occurs with multiplicity at
least 1 in H1(X0; C), and therefore also in H1(X0; Q).
Now consider the modified action of G on X0 as in Lemma 7.6. The modified
automorphism eg∗has the same eigenvalues as g∗on H1(X0; Q). It follows that
the isotypic decomposition Q Xρ →X0 for this modified G-action on X0 contains
a nontrivial factor Xtriv for the trivial representation. Proposition 7.10 implies
that Xtriv is symplectic, so we see that Xtriv has dimension at least 2, and this
implies that the trivial representation of ⟨g⟩occurs with multiplicity at least 4 in
H1(X0; Q).
7.3
Orlov’s conjecture for symplectic fourfolds
In this section, we will prove Conjecture 1.1 for smooth projective varieties of
dimension 4 admitting a holomorphic symplectic form, even if they do not satisfy
condition (∗). First, we will use the results of the previous section to understand
the different types of symplectic varieties that can exist in dimension 4, and then
we will use LLV algebras and the Verbitsky component to prove the conjecture.

Let V be a polarizable Q-Hodge structure of weight 2. Define eV = Q[0] ⊕
V ⊕Q[4], where V sits in degree 2. Define a Hodge structure on eV where the
two copies of Q are algebraic. Note that eV looks like the Hodge structure on the
even part of the cohomology of a smooth projective surface.

2. Suppose that there is a linear isomorphism f : eV →f
W which preserves the
Hodge grading (but not necessarily the normal grading). Then there also is an

W which preserves the Hodge bigrading.
Proof. This follows directly from the fact that the category of polarizable Hodge
structures is semisimple.
Similarly, if V is a polarizable Q-Hodge structure of weight 1, we can consider
V ⊕V (−1), where V (−1) is the Tate twist of V , and is a Hodge structure of
weight 3. We give V ⊕V (−1) the grading where V lives in degree 1 and V (−1)
in degree 3. Note that V ⊕V (−1) looks like the odd part of the cohomology of
a smooth projective surface.

Proof. This follows directly from the fact that the category of polarizable Hodge

We can now prove Orlov’s conjecture for symplectic fourfolds:
Theorem 7.17. Let X and Y be two smooth projective varieties of dimension 4
over C, both equipped with a holomorphic symplectic form, and assume that there
is a triangulated equivalence Φ: Db(X) ≃Db(Y ). Then there is an isomorphism
H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Y ; Q) preserving the Hodge bigrading.
Proof. By Theorem 2.62, we can write X = (Q
i Xi)/G and Y = (Q
j Yj)/H
as quotients of a product of abelian and hyperkähler varieties. Moreover, these
products are of one of the five possible types as given in Proposition 7.14.
First assume that X is of the first or the fifth type (so X is either a hyperkähler
or an abelian variety). Then Y is also a hyperkähler or abelian variety respectively
by [HNW11, Theorem 0.4], and then we already know that the conjecture is true
(either by [Tae23] or [Orl02] as seen in the introduction).
If X is of the second type, then llv(X; Q) ∼= so(eH(X1; Q)) × so(eH(X2; Q)).
Since dim H2(Xi; Q) = 22 for i = 1, 2, the variety X satisfies condition (∗).
Hence the same holds for Y , so the conjecture is true by Theorem 1.6.
Lastly, assume that X is of the third or fourth type. Since these are the only
two types in the classification where dim H1(X; Q) = 4, the variety Y must also
be of the third or fourth type by [PS11, Corollary B]. In both these cases, the Lie
algebra llv(X; Q) is a product of two simple factors by Theorem 3.78. However,
in both these cases, the LLV algebra has a simple factor of type D4, so we cannot
apply Theorem 1.6.
If X is of the form (X0 × X1)/G where G acts trivially on H1(X0; Q), and
therefore trivially on all of H•(X0; Q), then the cohomology of X factorizes as
H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(X0; Q) ⊗H•(X1; Q)G.
Let g1 = llv(X0; Q) and g2 = llvG(X1; Q), then llv(X; Q) ∼= g1 × g2 by The-
orem 3.78.
Moreover, note that H•(X0; Q) = Hev(X0; Q) ⊕Hodd(X0; Q) is a
direct sum of two absolutely irreducible g1-representations, and H•(X1; Q)G =
SHG(X1; Q) is an absolutely irreducible g2-representation.
If X is of the form X0/G, where X0 has the isotypic decomposition Xtriv ×
Xρ →X, where Xtriv and Xρ both have dimension 2, then we also have a
decomposition
H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Xtriv; Q) ⊗H•(Xρ; Q)G.
If we now let g1 = llv(Xtriv; Q) and g2 = llvG(Xρ; Q), then llv(X; Q) ∼= g1 × g2.
As above, we see that H•(X0; Q) = Hev(X0; Q) ⊕Hodd(X0; Q) is a direct sum of
two absolutely irreducible g1-representations, and H•(Xρ; Q)G = SHG(Xρ; Q) is
an absolutely irreducible g2-representation.

So, now suppose that X and Y are two symplectic fourfolds of the third or

ΦH,ev : Hev(X; Q) →Hev(Y ; Q) and ΦH,odd : Hodd(X; Q) →Hodd(Y ; Q).
We saw above that the LLV algebras of X and Y both are a product of two
simple factors, say llv(X; Q) ∼= g1 × g2 and llv(Y ; Q) ∼= h1 × h2. Furthermore,
Hev(X; Q) and Hodd(X; Q) both are a tensor product of absolutely irreducible
representations of g1 and g2, and similarly Hev(Y ; Q) and Hodd(Y ; Q) both are
a tensor product of absolutely irreducible representations of h1 and h2.

Remark 7.18. In particular, the above theorem implies the derived invariance
of the Hodge numbers for symplectic fourfolds. We should note here that this
derived invariance of the Hodge numbers also follows from a result by Abuaf
[Abu17]. Abuaf [Abu17, Theorem 1.3(4)] shows that for fourfolds, the Hodge
numbers h2,0 are derived invariants. One can then use the symmetries of the
Hodge diamond of a holomorphic symplectic variety, together with the derived
invariance of h1,0 [PS11, Corollary B], to show that all Hodge numbers are derived
invariants.
Remark 7.19. The proof also deals with the possibility of a derived equivalence
Db(X) ≃Db(Y ) where X is of the third type in the classification of Proposi-
tion 7.14 and Y is of the fourth type. I do not know of an example of such an
equivalence, but it would be interesting to know whether such a derived equiv-
alence exists. At least on the level of LLV algebras it seems possible. Indeed,
take X = (S × A)/G as in the introduction to this thesis (see just below The-
orem 1.6). To construct Y , we consider an abelian surface A, a 2-torsion point
a ∈A[2], and H = ⟨g⟩the cyclic group of order 4, acting on A × A by letting g
map (x, y) ∈A × A to (y, x + a). Then Y := (A × A)/H is of the fourth type in
Proposition 7.14, and the LLV algebras of X and Y both are a product of two
simple factors of type D4.
In the above proof, for the case where X = X1 × X2 is a product of two K3
surfaces, we also could have used the same argument as in the third and fourth

7.4
Summary
We now use the results of this chapter to state Theorem 1.6 in all forms proven
so far:
Theorem 7.21. Let X and Y be two smooth projective varieties over C, both
admitting a holomorphic symplectic form, and let Φ: Db(X) →Db(Y ) be a tri-
angulated equivalence.
Assume that at least one of the following is true:
• The dimension of X is 4.
• The following three properties hold:
– All simple factors of llv(X; Q) and llv(Y ; Q) of type D4 are of hyper-
kähler origin.
– All simple factors of llv(X; Q) and llv(Y ; Q) of type C2 are either all
of hyperkähler origin or all of abelian origin.
– All simple factors of llv(X; Q) and llv(Y ; Q) of type A3 are either all
of hyperkähler origin or all of abelian origin.

ENRIQUES MANIFOLDS
An Enriques surface is a surface which is not simply connected, and whose simply
connected covering is a K3 surface. The canonical bundle ωS of an Enriques sur-
face S is not trivial, but ω⊗2
S
is trivial. It is known that two Enriques surfaces that
are derived equivalent are actually isomorphic [BM01, Proposition 6.1]. There
are several ways in which one can generalize the notion of an Enriques surface
to higher dimensions, see for instance [OS11] and [BNWS11]. In this chapter, we
use the definition from [OS11]:
Definition 8.1. An Enriques variety is a smooth connected projective variety
X over C, which is not simply connected, and whose simply connected cover
e
X →X is a hyperkähler variety.
If X is an Enriques variety, then its fundamental group is cyclic, say of order
n, and the canonical bundle of X is torsion of order n, see [OS11, Lemma 2.3].
The hyperkähler covering e
X →X is then the canonical cover of X.
In this chapter, we prove Conjecture 1.1 for Enriques varieties:
Theorem 8.2. Let X and Y be two Enriques varieties. Let Φ: Db(X) →Db(Y )
be an equivalence of triangulated categories. Then for every i there is an isomor-
phism Hi(X; Q) ∼= Hi(Y ; Q) that preserves the Hodge structure.
Let n be the order of the canonical bundle ωX, and let G = Z/nZ. Then
ωY also has order n (see [Huy06, Proposition 4.1]), and we can write X ∼= e
X/G
and Y ∼= eY /G, where e
X and eY are the canonical covers of X and Y (in par-
ticular, e
X and eY are hyperkähler varieties). To prove the theorem, we will use
a result by Bridgeland-Maciocia [BM17] to lift the equivalence Φ to an equiva-
lence eΦ: Db( e
X) →Db(eY ). By [Tae23, Theorem D], we obtain an isomorphism

H•( e
X; Q) ∼= H•(eY ; Q) which preserves the Hodge bigrading. We will show that
there in fact exists such an isomorphism which is also equivariant with respect to
the G-actions. By taking G-invariants, we will obtain the desired isomorphism

Since we will use some details of the proof of [Tae23, Theorem D], we now

Taelman then uses ΨX and ΨY to show that there is a similitude φ: eH( e
X; Q) →

The actions of G on e
X and eY induce actions on the cohomology, the LLV
algebra, and so forth. In this chapter, we will show that the above construc-
tions of ΨX, φ and ψ are G-equivariant, and use this to show that the induced
automorphism ΨH is G-equivariant.
8.1
Equivariant Witt cancellation
In this section, we prove an equivariant version of the Witt cancellation by adapt-
ing the standard proof as given in [CMM17, Section 3]. This proof only works
when cancelling a subspace on which the group acts trivially, which suffices for
our application.
Definition 8.3. Let G be a finite group, let V be a G-representation over a field
k of characteristic unequal to 2, and let q be a quadratic form on V . We say that
(V, q) is a G-equivariant quadratic space if for all g ∈G and v ∈V we have
q(gv) = q(v).
Theorem 8.4. Let (V, q) be a G-equivariant nondegenerate quadratic space over
a field of characteristic different from 2, and let {e1, . . . , en} and {f1, . . . , fn} be
two q-orthogonal bases of V . Let m ≤n and assume that for all 1 ≤i ≤m
and g ∈G we have gei = ei, gfi = fi and q(ei) = q(fi). Then there exists a G-
equivariant isometry between the G-equivariant quadratic spaces ⟨em+1, . . . , en⟩
and ⟨fm+1, . . . , fn⟩.

8.2
Equivariant constructions
We will now show that all relevant constructions in the proof of [Tae23, The-
orem D] are G-equivariant. Examples of this are the element φ constructed in
[Tae23, Proposition 4.9], and the projection GSpin(V, q) →SO(V, q) for a G-
equivariant quadratic space V . We start with some linear algebra.
Lemma 8.5. Let V be a vector space over a field k of characteristic 0 and let
d ≥1 be an integer. Denote by f : V →Symd(V ) the map v 7→vd. Then for
v, w ∈V , we have f(v) = f(w) if and only if there is a λ ∈µd(k) with w = λv.
Proof. It is clear that f(v) = f(λv) for λ ∈µd(k). Conversely, suppose that
f(v) = f(w). Given a basis ⟨e1, . . . , en⟩of V , we know that Symd(V ) has a basis
given by the set of unordered products
{ei1 · · · ein : i1, . . . , in ∈{1, . . . , n}}.
If w is not a scalar multiple of v, then V has a basis with the first two elements
v and w, from which we can conclude that f(v) ̸= f(w). Otherwise, there is a
λ ∈k∗with v = λw. Then f(λv) = λdf(v), so the assumption that f(v) = f(w)
gives λd = 1, which finishes the proof.

gx = gf ′
x.
Since dρ0(fx) = f ′
x, we now conclude just as above
that dρ0(gfx) = gdρ0(fx).
Since the equality dρ0(gfx) = gdρ0(fx) holds for
a Zariski-dense collection of fx ∈so(eH(eY ; Q))−2, it must in fact hold for all
f ∈so(eH(eY ; Q))−2.
Since so(eH(eY ; Q)) is generated by its elements in degrees 2 and −2, we con-
clude that dρ0 is G-equivariant on all of so(eH(eY ; Q)), which finishes the proof.
In [Tae23, Lemma 5.1], Taelman constructs ρ from ρ0 by using the first short
exact sequence from Lemma 6.4, and writing down a representation of Gm,Q
which commutes with the image of ρ0.

Proof. The action of G on H•(eY ; Q) respects the grading, so the action written
down by Taelman in [Tae23, Lemma 5.1] is G-equivariant. Lemma 8.10 says that
the representation ρ0 : Spin(eH(eY ; Q)) →GL(H•(eY ; Q)) is also G-equivariant, so
it follows that ρ is G-equivariant.

The proof of Theorem 8.2 follows the proof of [Tae23, Theorem D], where we now
check that all relevant constructions are G-equivariant.
Proof of Theorem 8.2. Let n be the order of ωX, and let G = Z/nZ. By [Huy06,
Proposition 4.1], the canonical bundle of Y also has order n. Let eY be the canon-

implies that there is an automorphism µ: G →G and an equivalence
eΦ: Db( e
X) →Db(eY ),
with the property that g∗◦eΦ = eΦ ◦µ(g)∗for all g ∈G. By defining a new action
of G on e
X by precomposing the map G →Aut( e
X) with µ−1, we may assume µ
to be the identity. Then the induced map on cohomology eΦH is G-equivariant
due to the functioriality of the construction of eΦH in terms of eΦ.
There are actions of G on llv( e

and the isomorphism eΦllv : llv( e

By Lemmas 8.7 and 8.8, there exist a scalar λ ∈Q× and a G-equivariant

now claim that there is a G-equivariant Hodge isometry ψ ∈SO(eH(eY ; Q)) with

To see this, we use the same strategy as in [Tae23, Theorem 5.3], where we
now have to use our equivariant version of Witt cancellation. For completeness,
we spell out some details here.
Write eH(eY ; Q) = N ⊕T, where N is the algebraic part and T the tran-
scendental part. The subspaces N and T are orthogonal for the Mukai pairing,
since the Mukai pairing respects the Hodge structure. Recall that eH(eY ; Q) =
QαY ⊕H2(eY ; Q)⊕QβY , and we have αY , βY ∈N. Since φ: eH( e
X; Q) →eH(eY ; Q)
is an equivariant Hodge similitude, it maps the G-invariant hyperbolic plane
QαX ⊕QβX to a G-invariant hyperbolic plane V ⊆N. Then equivariant Witt
cancellation (Theorem 8.4) implies that there are a G-equivariant isometry ψN
of N and scalars s, t ∈Q× such that ψN ◦φ sends αX to sαY and βX to tβY .

8.4
Epilogue
Now, at the end of this chapter1, one glaring question remains: suppose X and Y
are smooth projective varieties with torsion canonical bundle, and whose canon-
ical covers e
X →X and eY →Y are varieties admitting a holomorphic symplectic
form (possibly satisfying condition (∗)). If there is an equivalence Φ: Db( e
X) ≃
Db(eY ), can we prove that there is an isomorphism H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Y ; Q) which
preserves the Hodge bigrading? I don’t know the answer to this question2, and
leave it as a direction for further research.
1In fact, the end of this thesis, except for the summary, the references and the index of
notation.
2Given the techniques developed so far, I guess that the answer is yes; at least under suitable
hypotheses such as e
X satisfying condition (∗)


## Page 165


SUMMARY
Varieties admitting a holomorphic symplectic form:
LLV algebras and derived equivalences
When doing geometry in two or three dimensions, we can often use our intuition,
and can study geometric objects by drawing or visualizing them. As soon as we
start to do geometry in higher dimensions, we can no longer easily visualize the
objects that we study, and it becomes harder to study their properties.
Modern mathematics has found solutions for this. Many ways have been found
to get a handle on higher-dimensional geometric objects. This is often done by
using simpler mathematical objects, which reflect some geometric information of
the geometric object we wish to study. We call such easier objects invariants.
A well-known example is the cohomology, which roughly counts the number of
holes of a shape.
This dissertation is in the area of algebraic geometry, where the geometric
objects which we study are so-called algebraic varieties. Algebraic varieties are
given as the solution sets to polynomial equations, and have a rich structure.
This rich structure leads to many interesting invariants of algebraic varieties.
These invariants carry information about the specific algebraic structure of the
variety. For example, the cohomology of an algebraic variety over C carries an
extra structure, the Hodge structure, which contains a lot of information about
the variety.
Another important invariant of an algebraic variety is the derived category.
This is a category which is built in a few steps from the category of coherent
sheaves on the variety.
It is possible that two different varieties have the same derived category. We
can then wonder how similar these two varieties are. For example, two varieties
with the same derived category have the same dimension. It is still unknown in
what sense the derived category determines the cohomology and Hodge structure.
An important conjecture in this direction is the following conjecture by Orlov:
Conjecture (Orlov). Let X and Y be two smooth projective varieties over C,


## Page 166


158
Summary
and assume that there is an equivalence Db(X) ≃Db(Y ) of triangulated cate-
gories. Then there is an isomorphism H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Y ; Q) which preserves the
grading and Hodge structure.
In this dissertation, I study a specific kind of varieties, namely smooth projec-
tive varieties over C which admit a holomorphic symplectic form. I prove Orlov’s
conjecture for many such varieties.
This result builds upon a result of Taelman [Tae23]. Using the hard Lefschetz
theorem, one can associate to a variety a Lie algebra (the LLV algebra, named
after Looijenga, Lunts and Verbitsky [LL97, Ver95]), and there is a representation
of this Lie algebra on the cohomology of the variety. Taelman has proved that for
varieties admitting a holomorphic symplectic form this Lie algebra is an invariant
of the derived category.
An important part of this thesis is the computation of the LLV algebra of
a general variety admitting a holomorphic symplectic form. Using this compu-
tation, I prove Orlov’s conjecture for many varieties admitting a holomorphic
symplectic form. Furthermore, I prove this conjecture for all 4-dimensional vari-
eties admitting a holomorphic symplectic form.


## Page 167


SAMENVATTING
Variëteiten met een holomorfe symplectische vorm:
LLV algebras en afgeleide equivalenties
Wanneer we meetkunde doen in twee of drie dimensies, kunnen we vaak ge-
bruik maken van onze intuïtie. We kunnen ons iets voorstellen bij de objecten
die we bestuderen, en kunnen er tekeningen of modellen van maken. Zodra we
meetkunde willen doen in hogere dimensies, verdwijnt de mogelijkheid om deze
objecten eenvoudig voor te stellen en wordt het lastiger om hun eigenschappen
te bestuderen.
De moderne wiskunde heeft hier echter oplossingen voor gevonden. Er zijn
veel manieren gevonden om grip te krijgen op hoger dimensionale meetkundige
objecten. Dit gaat meestal met behulp van simpelere objecten die informatie
bevatten over de meetkundige objecten die we bestuderen, en dit noemen we
invarianten. Een bekend voorbeeld is de cohomologie, die grof gezien het aantal
gaten van een meetkundig object telt.
Dit proefschrift speelt zich af in de algebraïsche meetkunde, waar de bestudeerde
meetkundige objecten zogeheten algebraïsche variëteiten zijn. Algebraïsche var-
iëteiten zijn gegeven als nulpuntsverzamelingen van polynomiale vergelijkingen,
en bezitten een rijke structuur. Dit komt onder andere tot uiting in de vele in-
teressante invarianten die men aan algebraïsche variëteiten kan toekennen. Deze
invarianten dragen informatie over de specifieke algebraïsche structuur van de
variëteit. De cohomologie van een gladde variëteit over C bijvoorbeeld draagt
nog een extra structuur, de zogenaamde Hodge structuur, die veel informatie
over de variëteit bevat.
Een andere belangrijke invariant van een algebraïsche variëteit is de afgeleide
categorie. Dit is een categorie die in een aantal stappen gebouwd wordt uit de
categorie van coherente schoven op de variëteit.
Het is mogelijk dat twee verschillende variëteiten dezelfde afgeleide categorie
hebben. We kunnen ons dan vragen in hoeverre deze variëteiten op elkaar lijken.
Twee variëteiten met dezelfde afgeleide categorie hebben bijvoorbeeld dezelfde di-


## Page 168


160
Samenvatting
mensie. Het is nog onbekend in welke mate de afgeleide categorie de cohomologie
en Hodge structuur precies bepaalt. Een belangrijk vermoeden in deze richting
is het vermoeden van Orlov:
Vermoeden (Orlov). Zij X en Y twee gladde projectieve variëteiten over C, en
neem aan dat er een equivalentie Db(X) ≃Db(Y ) van getrianguleerde categorieën
bestaat. Dan is er een isomorfisme H•(X; Q) ∼= H•(Y ; Q) dat de gradering en
Hodge structuur bewaart.
In dit proefschrift kijk ik naar een specifiek soort variëteiten, namelijk gladde
projectieve variëteiten over C met een holomorfe symplectische vorm. Voor een
groot deel van deze variëteiten bewijs ik dat Orlovs vermoeden inderdaad waar
is.
Dit resultaat bouwt voort op een resultaat van Taelman [Tae23]. Met be-
hulp van de harde Lefschetz-stelling kan men aan een variëteit een Lie alge-
bra toekennen (de LLV algebra, vernoemd naar Looijenga, Lunts en Verbitsky
[LL97, Ver95]), en deze Lie algebra heeft een representatie op de cohomologie.
Taelman heeft bewezen dat voor variëteiten met een holomorfe symplectische
vorm deze Lie algebra een invariant van de afgeleide categorie is.
Een belangrijk onderdeel van dit proefschrift is de berekening van de LLV
algebra van een algemene variëteit met een holomorfe symplectische vorm. Deze
berekening gebruik ik vervolgens om Orlovs vermoeden te bewijzen voor een
groot aantal variëteiten met een holomorfe symplectische vorm. Daarnaast laat
ik zien dat Orlovs vermoeden waar is voor alle 4-dimensionale variëteiten met
een holomorfe symplectische vorm.
